\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}(\widetilde{A}_{-1},\ldots,\widetilde{A}_{-r},\widetilde{A}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{A}_{r})=D(t_{1},\ldots,t_{r})E_{i_{1}}(a_{i_{1}})\cdots E_{i_{2r}}(a_{i_{2r}})

where

(3.15)

\displaystyle\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}^{*}(t_{j})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{A}_{j}^{\varepsilon_{j}}\widetilde{A}_{-j}^{-\varepsilon_{j}}

(3.16)

\displaystyle\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}^{*}(\overline{a}_{j})

\displaystyle=\prod_{k\in\widetilde{I}}\widetilde{A}_{k}^{-\varepsilon_{j}\widetilde{B}_{k,-j}},

and
\overline{a}_{j}=a_{j_{-}}a_{j_{+}}
for all
j\in[1,r]
, such that the maps
\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}
and
\widetilde{x}_{\mathbf{i}}
intertwine the maps
p_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{{\mathbf{i}}}}:\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{i}}}\to\mathcal{X}_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{i}}}
and
\pi:G^{u,v}/H\to G_{\Ad}^{u,v}/H_{\Ad}
, that is, we have
\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{i}}}
G^{u,v}/H
\mathcal{X}_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{i}}}
G_{\Ad}^{u,v}/H_{\Ad}
\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}
\pi
p_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{{\mathbf{i}}}}
\widetilde{x}_{\mathbf{i}}

Proof.

Firstly, we note from (
3.2
) that a generic element in
G_{\Ad}^{u,v}/H_{\Ad}
can be written as

\prod_{j=1}^{r}u_{j}^{\omega_{j}^{\vee}}\cdot E_{i_{1}}(b_{i_{1}})\cdots E_{i_{2r}}(b_{i_{2r}})

for some
b_{1},\ldots,b_{2r},u_{1},\ldots,u_{r}\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
. We need to show that

(3.17)

\displaystyle(\pi\circ\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}})^{*}(u_{j})

\displaystyle=(\widetilde{x}_{\mathbf{i}}\circ p_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{{\mathbf{i}}}})^{*}(u_{j}),\quad\text{and}

(3.18)

\displaystyle(\pi\circ\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}})^{*}(\overline{b}_{j})

\displaystyle=(\widetilde{x}_{\mathbf{i}}\circ p_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{{\mathbf{i}}}})^{*}(\overline{b}_{j}),

where
\overline{b}_{j}=b_{j_{-}}b_{j_{+}}
for all
j\in[1,r]
. To this end, we first note that we have
\alpha_{j}^{\vee}=\sum_{k=1}^{r}C_{j,k}\omega_{k}^{\vee}
for all
j\in[1,r]
. Thus, it follows that for all
j\in[1,r]
, we have

\pi^{*}(u_{j})=\prod_{k=1}^{r}t_{k}^{C_{k,j}},

and using (
3.15
), we have

(\pi\circ\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}})^{*}(u_{j})=\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}^{*}(\pi^{*}(u_{j}))=\prod_{k=1}^{r}\widetilde{A}_{k}^{\varepsilon_{k}C_{k,j}}\widetilde{A}_{-k}^{-\varepsilon_{k}C_{k,j}}.

On the other hand, we have

(\widetilde{x}_{\mathbf{i}}\circ p_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{{\mathbf{i}}}})^{*}(u_{j})=p_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{{\mathbf{i}}}}^{*}(\widetilde{x}_{\mathbf{i}}^{*}(u_{j}))=p_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{{\mathbf{i}}}}^{*}(\widetilde{X}_{j}\widetilde{X}_{-j})=\prod_{k=1}^{r}\widetilde{A}_{k}^{\widetilde{B}_{k,j}+\widetilde{B}_{k,-j}}\widetilde{A}_{-k}^{\widetilde{B}_{-k,j}+\widetilde{B}_{-k,-j}}

for all
j\in[1,r]
. Using equations (
3.4
)-(
3.7
), we can check case-by-case to deduce that we have
\widetilde{B}_{k,j}+\widetilde{B}_{k,-j}=\varepsilon_{k}C_{k,j}
and
\widetilde{B}_{-k,j}+\widetilde{B}_{-k,-j}=-\varepsilon_{k}C_{k,j}
for all
j,k\in[1,r]
. Consequently, it follows that (
3.17
) holds.

Next, it follows from equations (
3.15
) and (
3.16
) that we have

(\pi\circ\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}})^{*}(\overline{b}_{j})=\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}^{*}(\pi^{*}(\overline{b}_{j}))=\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}^{*}(\overline{a}_{j})=\prod_{k\in\widetilde{I}}\widetilde{A}_{k}^{-\varepsilon_{j}\widetilde{B}_{k,-j}}.

On the other hand, using the commutation relation
E_{\pm j}t^{\omega_{j}^{\vee}}=t^{\omega_{j}^{\vee}}E_{\pm j}(t^{\mp 1})
, we have

(\widetilde{x}_{\mathbf{i}}\circ p_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{{\mathbf{i}}}})^{*}(\overline{b}_{j})=p_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{{\mathbf{i}}}}^{*}(\widetilde{x}_{\mathbf{i}}^{*}(\overline{b}_{j}))=p_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{{\mathbf{i}}}}^{*}(\widetilde{X}_{-j}^{-\varepsilon_{j}})=\prod_{k\in\widetilde{I}}\widetilde{A}_{k}^{-\varepsilon_{j}\widetilde{B}_{k,-j}}.

Consequently, it follows that (
3.18
) holds, and this completes the proof.
∎

Remark
.

Here, we note that even though
\widetilde{x}_{\mathbf{i}}:\mathcal{X}_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{i}}}\to G_{\Ad}^{u,v}/H_{\Ad}
is an open immersion, the map
\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}:\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{i}}}\to G^{u,v}/H
is not an open immersion.

Proposition 3.13
.

Let
(u,v)
be a pair of Coxeter elements of
W
, and
\mathbf{i}
be a double reduced word for
(u,v)
. Then the map
\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}
is Poisson, with respect to the log-canonical Poisson structure on
\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{i}}}
as defined by (
3.13
), and the Poisson structure on
G^{u,v}/H
induced from the standard Poisson-Lie structure on
G
.

In order to prove Proposition
3.13
, we will need a few auxiliary lemmas.

Lemma 3.14
.

Let
(u,v)
be a pair of Coxeter elements, and
\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{2r})
be a double reduced word for
(u,v)
. Then with respect to the following factorization

D(t_{1},\ldots,t_{r})E_{i_{1}}(a_{i_{1}})\cdots E_{i_{2r}}(a_{i_{2r}})

of a generic element in
G^{u,v}/H
(up to conjugation), the Poisson brackets between the rational functions
\overline{a}_{j}=a_{j_{-}}a_{j_{+}}
and
t_{k}
,
j,k\in[1,r]
, on
G^{u,v}/H
are given by

(3.19)

\displaystyle\{\overline{a}_{j},\overline{a}_{k}\}_{u,v}

\displaystyle=d_{j}^{\prime}\widetilde{B}_{-j,-k}\varepsilon_{j}\varepsilon_{k}\overline{a}_{j}\overline{a}_{k},

(3.20)

\displaystyle\{\overline{a}_{j},t_{k}\}_{u,v}

\displaystyle=d_{j}^{\prime}\delta_{j,k}\overline{a}_{j}t_{k},\quad\text{and}

(3.21)

\displaystyle\{t_{j},t_{k}\}_{u,v}

\displaystyle=0

for all
j,k\in[1,r]
.

Lemma
3.14
follows from equations (
2.4
)-(
2.6
), (
3.4
), along with a case-by-case analysis. We shall leave the details to the reader.

Lemma 3.15
.

Let
(u,v)\in W\times W
be a pair of Coxeter elements,
j,\ell\in[1,r]
, and
\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{2r})
,
\mathbf{i}^{\prime}=(i_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,i_{2r}^{\prime})
be double reduced words for
(u,v)
that differ by swapping two adjacent indices
k
and
k+1
differing only by a sign, that is, we have
i_{k}=-i_{k+1}
, and

i_{\ell}^{\prime}=\begin{cases}i_{k+1}&\text{if }\ell=k,\\
i_{k}&\text{if }\ell=k+1,\\
i_{\ell}&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Suppose that we have

\widetilde{\Lambda}_{i,j}-\widetilde{\Lambda}_{-i,j}-\widetilde{\Lambda}_{i,-j}+\widetilde{\Lambda}_{-i,-j}=0

for all
i,j\in[1,r]
. Then we also have

\widetilde{\Lambda}_{i,j}^{\prime}-\widetilde{\Lambda}_{-i,j}^{\prime}-\widetilde{\Lambda}_{i,-j}^{\prime}+\widetilde{\Lambda}_{-i,-j}^{\prime}=0

for all
i,j\in[1,r]
.

Proof.

Let
\ell=|i_{k}|
. Then we have
[
BZ05
, (3.2), (3.4)]

(3.22)

\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\prime}=E^{T}\widetilde{\Lambda}E,

where
E
is the
2r\times 2r
matrix with row and column indices
-1,\ldots,-r,1,\ldots,r
with entries defined by
[
BZ05
, (3.2),(3.4)]

E_{p,q}=\begin{cases}\delta_{p,q}&\text{if }q\neq-\ell,\\
-1&\text{if }p=q=-\ell,\\
[\varepsilon_{\ell}\widetilde{B}_{p,-\ell}^{\prime}]_{+}&\text{if }p\neq q=-\ell\end{cases}

for all
p,q\in\widetilde{I}
. Then it is easy to see from the definition of
E_{p,q}
that if
i,j\in[1,r]\setminus\{|\ell|\}
, then we have
\widetilde{\Lambda}_{\pm i,\pm j}^{\prime}=\widetilde{\Lambda}_{\pm i,\pm j}
and
\widetilde{\Lambda}_{\pm i,\mp j}^{\prime}=\widetilde{\Lambda}_{\pm i,\mp j}
, and thus Lemma
3.15
holds in the case where
i,j\in[1,r]\setminus\{|\ell|\}
. Consequently, we only need to consider the case where either
i=|\ell|
or
j=|\ell|
. As
\widetilde{\Lambda}
and
\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\prime}
are skew-symmetric, it suffices to consider the latter case, and we may also assume that
i\neq|\ell|
.

Now, we have
\varepsilon_{\ell}\widetilde{B}_{-p,-\ell}=C_{p,\ell}\delta_{p_{-}<\ell_{-}<p_{+}}
and
\varepsilon_{\ell}\widetilde{B}_{p,-\ell}=C_{p,\ell}(\delta_{p,\ell}+\delta_{\ell_{-}<p_{+}}+\delta_{p_{+}<\ell_{-}}
for all
p\in[1,r]\setminus\{|\ell|\}
. This implies that for all
q\in\widetilde{I}
, we have
\varepsilon_{\ell}\widetilde{B}_{q,-\ell}^{\prime}>0
if and only if
q=\ell
. Thus, by (
3.22
), along with the definition of
E_{p,q}
, it follows that we have

(3.23)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Lambda}_{i,\ell}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\Lambda}_{i,\ell},\quad\widetilde{\Lambda}_{-i,\ell}=\widetilde{\Lambda}_{-i,\ell},

(3.24)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Lambda}_{i,-\ell}

\displaystyle=-\widetilde{\Lambda}_{i,-\ell}+2\widetilde{\Lambda}_{i,\ell},\quad\widetilde{\Lambda}_{-i,-\ell}=-\widetilde{\Lambda}_{-i,-\ell}+2\widetilde{\Lambda}_{-i,\ell}.

By (
3.23
) and (
3.24
), it follows that Lemma
3.15
holds in the case where where either
i=|\ell|
or
j=|\ell|
. This completes the proof of Lemma
3.15
.
∎

Corollary 3.16
.

Let
(u,v)
be a pair of Coxeter elements, and
\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{2r})
be a double reduced word for
(u,v)
. Then for all
i,j\in[1,r]
, we have

\widetilde{\Lambda}_{i,j}-\widetilde{\Lambda}_{-i,j}-\widetilde{\Lambda}_{i,-j}+\widetilde{\Lambda}_{-i,-j}=0.

Proof.

We will proceed in several steps. Firstly, we will show that Corollary
3.16
holds in the case where
u=v
and
\mathbf{i}
is unmixed, in which case we have
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{i}}=\widetilde{\Sigma}_{(u,u)}
. In this case, the entries of
\widetilde{\Lambda}_{\mathbf{i}}
are given by

\widetilde{\Lambda}_{i,j}=\widetilde{\Lambda}_{-i,-j}=0,\quad\widetilde{\Lambda}_{-i,j}=-\widetilde{\Lambda}_{i,-j}=d_{i}^{\prime}(C^{-1})_{i,j}

for all
i,j\in[1,r]
, from which we see that Corollary
3.16
holds.

Next, we will show that Corollary
3.16
holds in the case where
\mathbf{i}
is unmixed, in which case we have
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{i}}=\widetilde{\Sigma}_{(u,v)}
. By Lemma
3.10
and Corollary
3.11
, there exist
j_{1},\ldots,j_{m}\in[1,r]
and
n_{1},\ldots,n_{m}\in\{\pm 1\}
, such that

\widetilde{\Sigma}_{(u,v)}=(\tau_{j_{m}}^{n_{m}}\circ\cdots\circ\tau_{j_{1}}^{n_{1}})(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{(u,u)}).

We shall proceed by induction on
m
that Corollary
3.16
holds for all
i,j\in[1,r]
, with the base case
m=0
being trivial. Suppose that Corollary
3.16
holds for the case
m=k-1
, where
k\geq 1
. We would like to show that Corollary
3.16
holds for the case
m=k
as well. We let
(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})
be a pair of Coxeter elements satisfying

\widetilde{\Sigma}_{(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})}=(\tau_{j_{k-1}}^{n_{k-1}}\circ\cdots\circ\tau_{j_{1}}^{n_{1}})(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{(u,u)}),

so that we have
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{(u,v)}=\tau_{j_{k}}^{n_{k}}(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})})
. As
\tau_{j_{k}}
is the composition of the permutation
\sigma_{j_{k}}
of
\widetilde{I}
, followed by a cluster mutation at
-j_{k}
, it follows from Lemma
3.15
, along with the induction hypothesis, that Corollary
3.16
holds for
(u,v)
. Consequently, it follows that Corollary
3.16
holds in the case where
\mathbf{i}
is unmixed.

Finally, if
\mathbf{i}
is any double reduced word for
(u,v)
, then there exists an unmixed double reduced word
\mathbf{i}^{\prime}
for
(u,v)
, and
k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}\in\{\pm 1,\ldots,\pm r\}
, such that

\widetilde{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{i}}=(\sigma_{-k_{n}}\circ\cdots\circ\sigma_{-k_{1}})(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}).

Now, by a similar argument as above, we can prove by induction on
n
that Corollary
3.16
holds for all
i,j\in[1,r]
. This completes the proof of Corollary
3.16
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.13
.

As
\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}
is a dominant map, it suffices to show that

(3.25)

\displaystyle\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}^{*}(\{\overline{a}_{j},\overline{a}_{k}\}_{u,v})

\displaystyle=\{\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}^{*}(\overline{a}_{j}),\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}^{*}(\overline{a}_{k})\},

(3.26)

\displaystyle\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}^{*}(\{\overline{a}_{j},t_{k}\}_{u,v})

\displaystyle=\{\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}^{*}(\overline{a}_{j}),\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}^{*}(t_{k})\},\quad\text{and}

(3.27)

\displaystyle\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}^{*}(\{t_{j},t_{k}\}_{u,v})

\displaystyle=\{\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}^{*}(t_{j}),\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}^{*}(t_{k})\}

for all
j,k\in[1,r]
. Firstly, by (
3.16
) and (
3.19
), we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}^{*}(\{\overline{a}_{j},\overline{a}_{k}\}_{u,v})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}^{*}(d_{j}^{\prime}\widetilde{B}_{-j,-k}\varepsilon_{j}\varepsilon_{k}\overline{a}_{j}\overline{a}_{k})

\displaystyle=d_{j}^{\prime}\widetilde{B}_{-j,-k}\varepsilon_{j}\varepsilon_{k}\prod_{i\in\widetilde{I}}\widetilde{A}_{i}^{-\varepsilon_{j}\widetilde{B}_{i,-j}-\varepsilon_{k}\widetilde{B}_{i,-k}},

\displaystyle\{\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}^{*}(\overline{a}_{j}),\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}^{*}(\overline{a}_{k})\}

\displaystyle=\left\{\prod_{i\in\widetilde{I}}\widetilde{A}_{i}^{-\varepsilon_{j}\widetilde{B}_{i,-j}},\prod_{\ell\in\widetilde{I}}^{r}\widetilde{A}_{\ell}^{-\varepsilon_{k}\widetilde{B}_{\ell,-k}}\right\}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{j}\varepsilon_{k}\left(\sum_{i,\ell\in\widetilde{I}}\widetilde{B}_{i,-j}\widetilde{\Lambda}_{i,\ell}\widetilde{B}_{\ell,-k}\right)\prod_{i\in\widetilde{I}}\widetilde{A}_{i}^{-\varepsilon_{j}\widetilde{B}_{i,-j}-\varepsilon_{k}\widetilde{B}_{i,-k}}

\displaystyle=-\varepsilon_{j}\varepsilon_{k}\left(\sum_{i\in\widetilde{I}}\widetilde{B}_{i,-j}\widetilde{D}_{i,-k}\right)\prod_{i\in\widetilde{I}}\widetilde{A}_{i}^{-\varepsilon_{j}\widetilde{B}_{i,-j}-\varepsilon_{k}\widetilde{B}_{i,-k}}

\displaystyle=-\varepsilon_{j}\varepsilon_{k}d_{k}^{\prime}\widetilde{B}_{-k,-j}\prod_{i\in\widetilde{I}}\widetilde{A}_{i}^{-\varepsilon_{j}\widetilde{B}_{i,-j}-\varepsilon_{k}\widetilde{B}_{i,-k}}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{j}\varepsilon_{k}d_{j}^{\prime}\widetilde{B}_{-j,-k}\prod_{i\in\widetilde{I}}\widetilde{A}_{i}^{-\varepsilon_{j}\widetilde{B}_{i,-j}-\varepsilon_{k}\widetilde{B}_{i,-k}},

and so (
3.25
) holds. Next, by (
3.15
), (
3.16
) and (
3.20
), we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}^{*}(\{\overline{a}_{j},t_{k}\}_{u,v})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}^{*}(d_{j}^{\prime}\delta_{j,k}\overline{a}_{j}t_{k})

\displaystyle=d_{j}^{\prime}\delta_{j,k}\widetilde{A}_{j}^{\varepsilon_{j}}\widetilde{A}_{-j}^{-\varepsilon_{j}}\prod_{i\in\widetilde{I}}\widetilde{A}_{i}^{-\varepsilon_{k}\widetilde{B}_{i,-k}}

\displaystyle\{\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}^{*}(\overline{a}_{j}),\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}^{*}(t_{k})\}

\displaystyle=\left\{\prod_{i\in\widetilde{I}}\widetilde{A}_{i}^{-\varepsilon_{j}\widetilde{B}_{i,-j}},\widetilde{A}_{k}^{\varepsilon_{k}}\widetilde{A}_{-k}^{-\varepsilon_{k}}\right\}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{j}\varepsilon_{k}\left(\sum_{i\in\widetilde{I}}(\widetilde{B}_{i,-j}\widetilde{\Lambda}_{i,-k}-\widetilde{B}_{i,-j}\widetilde{\Lambda}_{i,k})\right)\widetilde{A}_{j}^{\varepsilon_{j}}\widetilde{A}_{-j}^{-\varepsilon_{j}}\prod_{i\in\widetilde{I}}\widetilde{A}_{i}^{-\varepsilon_{k}\widetilde{B}_{i,-k}}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{j}\varepsilon_{k}(\widetilde{D}_{-j,-k}-\widetilde{D}_{-j,k})\widetilde{A}_{j}^{\varepsilon_{j}}\widetilde{A}_{-j}^{-\varepsilon_{j}}\prod_{i\in\widetilde{I}}\widetilde{A}_{i}^{-\varepsilon_{k}\widetilde{B}_{i,-k}}

\displaystyle=d_{j}^{\prime}\delta_{j,k}\widetilde{A}_{j}^{\varepsilon_{j}}\widetilde{A}_{-j}^{-\varepsilon_{j}}\prod_{i\in\widetilde{I}}\widetilde{A}_{i}^{-\varepsilon_{k}\widetilde{B}_{i,-k}},

which implies that (
3.26
) holds. Finally, by (
3.15
) and (
3.21
), we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}^{*}(\{t_{j},t_{k}\}_{u,v})

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\{\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}^{*}(t_{j}),\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}^{*}(t_{k})\}

\displaystyle=\left\{\widetilde{A}_{j}^{\varepsilon_{j}}\widetilde{A}_{-j}^{-\varepsilon_{j}},\widetilde{A}_{k}^{\varepsilon_{k}}\widetilde{A}_{-k}^{-\varepsilon_{k}}\right\}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{j}\varepsilon_{k}(\widetilde{\Lambda}_{j,k}-\widetilde{\Lambda}_{-j,k}-\widetilde{\Lambda}_{j,-k}+\widetilde{\Lambda}_{-j,-k})\widetilde{A}_{j}^{\varepsilon_{j}}\widetilde{A}_{-j}^{-\varepsilon_{j}}\widetilde{A}_{k}^{\varepsilon_{k}}\widetilde{A}_{-k}^{-\varepsilon_{k}}

\displaystyle=0,

where the last equality follows from Corollary
3.16
. So (
3.27
) holds as well, and thus the map
\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}
is Poisson as desired.
∎

4.
Generalized Bäcklund-Darboux transformations

Our goal in this section is to construct generalized Bäcklund-Darboux transformations between two quotients
G^{u,v}/H
and
G^{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}/H
of double Bruhat cells for any pairs of Coxeter elements
(u,v)
and
(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})
, and show that these transformations preserve Hamiltonian flows generated by the trace function of any representation of the simple Lie group, which would then show that the family of Coxeter-Toda systems on a simple Lie group forms a single cluster integrable system. These transformations arise naturally as a consequence of Lemma
3.10
and Corollary
3.11
, where we have shown that the two seeds
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{(u,v)}
and
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})}
are mutation-equivalent to each other, up to a permutation of indexing sets. These transformations were constructed in
[
GSV11
]
in the case where
G=SL_{r+1}(\mathbb{C})
. Our goal here is to construct these transformations for the other finite Dynkin types.

To make our notations and constructions more consistent with the notations used in
[
GSV11
]
, we will need a few notations. We will write
\widetilde{a}_{(u,v)}=\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}
where
\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{2r})
is any unmixed double reduced word for a pair
(u,v)
of Coxeter elements in
W
. By Proposition
3.6
,
\widetilde{a}_{(u,v)}
is independent of the choice of an unmixed double reduced word for
(u,v)
, and in particular,
\widetilde{a}_{(u,v)}
is well-defined.

Next, we note that since
\mathbf{i}
is unmixed, it follows that the map
\widetilde{a}_{(u,v)}:\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{(u,v)}}\to G^{u,v}/H
can be rewritten as

(4.1)

\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}(\widetilde{A}_{-1},\ldots,\widetilde{A}_{-r},\widetilde{A}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{A}_{r})=E_{i_{1}}(1)\cdots E_{i_{r}}(1)D(t_{1},\ldots,t_{r})E_{i_{r+1}}(c_{i_{r+1}})\cdots E_{i_{2r}}(c_{i_{2r}})

where

(4.2)

\displaystyle\widetilde{a}_{(u,v)}^{*}(t_{j})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{A}_{-j}\widetilde{A}_{j}^{-1}

(4.3)

\displaystyle\widetilde{a}_{(u,v)}^{*}(c_{j})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{a}_{(u,v)}^{*}\left(\overline{a}_{j}\prod_{i=1}^{r}t_{i}^{-C_{i,j}}\right)=\prod_{k\in\widetilde{I}}\widetilde{A}_{k}^{-\widetilde{B}_{k,j}}

for all
j,k\in[1,r]
, and
\widetilde{B}_{i,j}
(
i,j\in\widetilde{I}
) are the entries of
\widetilde{B}_{(u,v)}
given by (
3.8
)-(
3.10
).

4.1.
The elementary generalized Bäcklund-Darboux transformations

Our first step is to construct generalized Bäcklund-Darboux transformations between
G^{u,v}/H
and
G^{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}/H
in the case where
(u,v),(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})
satisfy the hypotheses of Lemma
3.10
, so that we have
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})}=\tau_{k}(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{(u,v)})=(\mu_{-k}\circ\sigma_{k})(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{(u,v)})
.
As in
[
GSV11
, Section 6.1]
, we will show that the corresponding transformation from
G^{u,v}/H
to
G^{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}/H
can be realized as the composition of an appropriate conjugation map from
G^{u,v}/H
to
G^{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}/H
, followed by a refactorization relation on
G^{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}/H
.

Before we construct these generalized Bäcklund-Darboux transformations, we will start off with the construction of the conjugation map from
G^{u,v}/H
to
G^{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}/H
, followed by the refactorization relation on
G^{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}/H
.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
(u,v),(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})\in W\times W
be pairs of Coxeter elements. Let
\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{2r})
,
\mathbf{i}^{\prime}=(i_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,i_{2r}^{\prime})
be double reduced words for
(u,v)
and
(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})
, such that
i_{2r}^{\prime}=i_{1}
and
i_{j}^{\prime}=i_{j+1}
for all
j\in[1,2r-1]
, and
k=|i_{1}|
. Then the map
\sigma_{k}:\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{i}}}\to\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}}
induces a birational Poisson map
C_{i_{1},-}:G^{u,v}/H\to G^{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}/H
given by

(4.4)

D(t_{1},\ldots,t_{r})E_{i_{1}}(a_{i_{1}})\cdots E_{i_{2r}}(a_{i_{2r}})\mapsto D(t_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,t_{r}^{\prime})E_{i_{1}^{\prime}}(a_{i_{1}^{\prime}}^{\prime})\cdots E_{i_{2r}^{\prime}}(a_{i_{2r}^{\prime}}^{\prime}),

where

(4.5)

\displaystyle C_{i_{1},-}^{*}(t_{j}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=t_{j}

(4.6)

\displaystyle C_{i_{1},-}^{*}(\overline{a}_{j}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\overline{a}_{j}\prod_{i=1}^{r}t_{i}^{\delta_{j,k}\varepsilon_{k}C_{i,k}},

and
\overline{a}_{j}=a_{j_{-}}a_{j_{+}}
,
\overline{a}_{j}^{\prime}=a_{j_{-}}^{\prime}a_{j_{+}}^{\prime}
for all
j\in[1,r]
, with its inverse
C_{i_{2r},+}:G^{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}/H\to G^{u,v}/H
given by

(4.7)

\displaystyle C_{i_{2r},+}^{*}(t_{j})

\displaystyle=t_{j}^{\prime}

(4.8)

\displaystyle C_{i_{2r},+}^{*}(\overline{a}_{j})

\displaystyle=\overline{a}_{j}^{\prime}\prod_{i=1}^{r}t_{i}^{\prime\delta_{j,k}\varepsilon_{k}^{\prime}C_{i,k}},

such that the maps
\sigma_{k}
and
C_{i_{1},-}
intertwine the maps
\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}:\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{i}}}\to G^{u,v}/H
and
\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}:\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}}\to G^{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}/H
, that is, we have
\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{i}}}
\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}}
G^{u,v}/H
G^{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}/H
\sigma_{k}
\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}
\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}
C_{i_{1},-}

Proof.

Firstly, we will show that
\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}\circ\sigma_{k}=C_{i_{1},-}\circ\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}
, by showing that

(4.9)

\displaystyle(\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}\circ\sigma_{k})^{*}(t_{j}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(C_{i_{1},-}\circ\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}})^{*}(t_{j}^{\prime}),\quad\text{and}

(4.10)

\displaystyle(\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}\circ\sigma_{k})^{*}(\overline{a}_{j}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(C_{i_{1},-}\circ\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}})^{*}(\overline{a}_{j}^{\prime}),

for all
j\in[1,r]
. To this end, we first note that we have
\varepsilon_{j}^{\prime}=\varepsilon_{j}
and
\sigma_{k}^{*}(\widetilde{A}_{\pm j}^{\prime})=\widetilde{A}_{\pm j}
for all
j\in[1,r]\setminus\{k\}
, and we have
\varepsilon_{k}^{\prime}=-\varepsilon_{k}
and
\sigma_{k}^{*}(\widetilde{A}_{\pm k}^{\prime})=\widetilde{A}_{\mp k}
. This implies that for all
j\in[1,r]\setminus\{k\}
, we have

\displaystyle(\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}\circ\sigma_{k})^{*}(t_{j}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sigma_{k}^{*}(\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}^{*}(t_{j}^{\prime}))=\sigma_{k}^{*}(\widetilde{A}_{j}^{\prime\varepsilon_{j}^{\prime}}\widetilde{A}_{-j}^{-\varepsilon_{j}^{\prime}})=\widetilde{A}_{j}^{\varepsilon_{j}}\widetilde{A}_{-j}^{-\varepsilon_{j}},

\displaystyle(C_{i_{1},-}\circ\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}})^{*}(t_{j}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}^{*}(C_{i_{1},-}^{*}(t_{j}^{\prime}))=\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}^{*}(t_{j})=\widetilde{A}_{j}^{\varepsilon_{j}}\widetilde{A}_{-j}^{-\varepsilon_{j}},

\displaystyle(\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}\circ\sigma_{k})^{*}(t_{k}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sigma_{k}^{*}(\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}^{*}(t_{k}^{\prime}))=\sigma_{k}^{*}(\widetilde{A}_{k}^{\prime\varepsilon_{k}^{\prime}}\widetilde{A}_{-k}^{-\varepsilon_{k}^{\prime}})=\widetilde{A}_{-k}^{-\varepsilon_{k}}\widetilde{A}_{k}^{\varepsilon_{k}},

\displaystyle(C_{i_{1},-}\circ\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}})^{*}(t_{k}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}^{*}(C_{i_{1},-}^{*}(t_{k}^{\prime}))=\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}^{*}(t_{k})=\widetilde{A}_{k}^{\varepsilon_{k}}\widetilde{A}_{-k}^{-\varepsilon_{k}},

from which we see that (
4.9
) holds. Next, we note that we have
\widetilde{B}_{\sigma_{k}(p),\sigma_{k}(q)}^{\prime}=\widetilde{B}_{p,q}
for all
p,q\in\widetilde{I}
. Thus, it follows that for all
j\in[1,r]\setminus\{k\}
, we have

\displaystyle(\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}\circ\sigma_{k})^{*}(\overline{a}_{j}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sigma_{k}^{*}(\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}^{*}(\overline{a}_{j}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\sigma_{k}^{*}\left(\widetilde{A}_{k}^{\prime-\varepsilon_{j}^{\prime}\widetilde{B}_{k,-j}^{\prime}}\widetilde{A}_{-k}^{\prime-\varepsilon_{j}^{\prime}\widetilde{B}_{-k,-j}^{\prime}}\prod_{i\in[1,r]\setminus\{k\}}\widetilde{A}_{i}^{\prime-\varepsilon_{j}^{\prime}\widetilde{B}_{i,-j}^{\prime}}\widetilde{A}_{-i}^{\prime-\varepsilon_{j}^{\prime}\widetilde{B}_{-i,-j}^{\prime}}\right)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{A}_{-k}^{-\varepsilon_{j}\widetilde{B}_{-k,-j}}\widetilde{A}_{k}^{-\varepsilon_{j}\widetilde{B}_{k,-j}}\prod_{i\in[1,r]\setminus\{k\}}\widetilde{A}_{i}^{-\varepsilon_{j}\widetilde{B}_{i,-j}}\widetilde{A}_{-i}^{-\varepsilon_{j}\widetilde{B}_{-i,-j}}

\displaystyle=\prod_{i\in\widetilde{I}}\widetilde{A}_{i}^{-\varepsilon_{j}\widetilde{B}_{i,-j}},

\displaystyle(C_{i_{1},-}\circ\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}})^{*}(\overline{a}_{j}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}^{*}(C_{i_{1},-}^{*}(\overline{a}_{j}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}^{*}(\overline{a}_{j})

\displaystyle=\prod_{i\in\widetilde{I}}\widetilde{A}_{i}^{-\varepsilon_{j}\widetilde{B}_{i,-j}},

from which we deduce that (
4.10
) holds for all
j\in[1,r]\setminus\{k\}
.

Finally, we observe that we have

\displaystyle-\varepsilon_{k}^{\prime}\widetilde{B}_{j,-k}^{\prime}+\varepsilon_{k}\widetilde{B}_{j,-k}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{k}(\widetilde{B}_{j,k}+\widetilde{B}_{j,-k})=\varepsilon_{j}\varepsilon_{k}C_{j,k},

\displaystyle-\varepsilon_{k}^{\prime}\widetilde{B}_{-j,-k}^{\prime}+\varepsilon_{k}\widetilde{B}_{-j,-k}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{k}(\widetilde{B}_{-j,k}+\widetilde{B}_{-j,k})=-\varepsilon_{j}\varepsilon_{k}C_{j,k}

for all
j\in[1,r]\setminus\{k\}
. Consequently, we have

\displaystyle(\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}\circ\sigma_{k})^{*}(\overline{a}_{k}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sigma_{k}^{*}(\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}^{*}(\overline{a}_{k}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\sigma_{k}^{*}\left(\widetilde{A}_{k}^{\prime-2}\prod_{i\in[1,r]\setminus\{k\}}\widetilde{A}_{i}^{\prime-\varepsilon_{k}^{\prime}\widetilde{B}_{i,-k}^{\prime}}\widetilde{A}_{-i}^{\prime-\varepsilon_{k}^{\prime}\widetilde{B}_{-i,-k}^{\prime}}\right)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{A}_{-k}^{-2}\prod_{i\in[1,r]\setminus\{k\}}\widetilde{A}_{i}^{-\varepsilon_{k}\widetilde{B}_{i,-k}}\widetilde{A}_{-i}^{-\varepsilon_{k}\widetilde{B}_{-i,-k}}\prod_{i\in[1,r]\setminus\{k\}}\widetilde{A}_{i}^{\varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{k}C_{i,k}}\widetilde{A}_{-i}^{-\varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{k}C_{i,k}}

\displaystyle=\prod_{i\in[1,r]}\widetilde{A}_{i}^{-\varepsilon_{k}\widetilde{B}_{i,-k}}\widetilde{A}_{-i}^{-\varepsilon_{k}\widetilde{B}_{-i,-k}}\prod_{i\in[1,r]}\widetilde{A}_{i}^{\varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{k}C_{i,k}}\widetilde{A}_{-i}^{-\varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{k}C_{i,k}},

\displaystyle(C_{i_{1},-}\circ\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}})^{*}(\overline{a}_{k}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}^{*}(C_{i_{1},-}^{*}(\overline{a}_{k}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}^{*}\left(\overline{a}_{k}\prod_{i=1}^{r}t_{i}^{\varepsilon_{k}C_{i,k}}\right)

\displaystyle=\prod_{i\in[1,r]}\widetilde{A}_{i}^{-\varepsilon_{k}\widetilde{B}_{i,-k}}\widetilde{A}_{-i}^{-\varepsilon_{k}\widetilde{B}_{-i,-k}}\prod_{i\in[1,r]}\widetilde{A}_{i}^{\varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{k}C_{i,k}}\widetilde{A}_{-i}^{-\varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{k}C_{i,k}},

from which we deduce that (
4.10
) holds for
j=k
.

Next, we need to show that
C_{i_{1},-}
is Poisson. As
C_{i_{1},-}
is a dominant map, it suffices to show that

(4.11)

\displaystyle C_{i_{1},-}^{*}(\{\overline{a}_{i},\overline{a}_{j}\}_{u,v})

\displaystyle=\{C_{i_{1},-}^{*}(\overline{a}_{i}),C_{i_{1},-}^{*}(\overline{a}_{j})\},

(4.12)

\displaystyle C_{i_{1},-}^{*}(\{\overline{a}_{i},t_{j}\}_{u,v})

\displaystyle=\{C_{i_{1},-}^{*}(\overline{a}_{i}),C_{i_{1},-}^{*}(t_{j})\},\quad\text{and}

(4.13)

\displaystyle C_{i_{1},-}^{*}(\{t_{i},t_{j}\}_{u,v})

\displaystyle=\{C_{i_{1},-}^{*}(t_{i}),C_{i_{1},-}^{*}(t_{j})\}

for all
j,k\in[1,r]
. It is easy to see using (
3.20
)-(
3.21
) and (
4.5
)-(
4.6
) that (
4.12
) and (
4.13
) holds trivially, while (
4.11
) follows from the fact that we have
\widetilde{B}_{-i,-j}^{\prime}=\widetilde{B}_{i,j}
, as well as
\widetilde{B}_{-j,k}+\widetilde{B}_{-j,-k}=-\varepsilon_{j}C_{j,k}
for all
j\in[1,r]
. We will omit the details here.

∎

When
\mathbf{i}
is unmixed, we see that the map
C_{i_{1},-}:G^{u,v}/H\to G^{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}/H
is given by the map

\displaystyle\quad E_{i_{1}}(1)\cdots E_{i_{r}}(1)D(t_{1},\ldots,t_{r})E_{i_{r+1}}(c_{i_{r+1}})\cdots E_{i_{2r}}(c_{i_{2r}})

(4.14)

\displaystyle\mapsto E_{i_{2}}(1)\cdots E_{i_{r}}(1)D(t_{1},\ldots,t_{r})E_{i_{r+1}}(c_{i_{r+1}})\cdots E_{i_{2r}}(c_{i_{2r}})E_{i_{1}}(1),

or equivalently, the birational map
C_{i_{1},-}
is given by conjugation by
E_{i_{1}}(-1)
. Indeed, this follows from the fact that if

\displaystyle\quad\,E_{i_{2}}(1)\cdots E_{i_{r}}(1)D(t_{1},\ldots,t_{r})E_{i_{r+1}}(c_{i_{r+1}})\cdots E_{i_{2r}}(c_{i_{2r}})E_{i_{1}}(1)

\displaystyle=D(t_{1},\ldots,t_{r})E_{i_{2}}(a_{i_{2}}^{\prime})\cdots E_{i_{2r}}(a_{i_{2r}}^{\prime})E_{i_{1}}(1),

then we have
\overline{a}_{k}^{\prime}=c_{k}
and
\overline{a}_{j}^{\prime}=c_{j}\prod_{i=1}^{r}t_{i}^{-C_{i,j}}
for all
j\neq k
. Together with (
4.3
), we have

C_{i_{1},-}^{*}(\overline{a}_{j}^{\prime})=\overline{a}_{j}\prod_{i=1}^{r}t_{i}^{-\delta_{j,k}C_{i,k}}

for all
j\in[1,r]
, which coincides with (
4.5
), owing to the fact that
\mathbf{i}
is unmixed. Similarly, when
\mathbf{i}^{\prime}
is unmixed, we see that the map
C_{i_{2r},+}:G^{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}/H\to G^{u,v}/H
is given by the map

\displaystyle\quad E_{i_{1}^{\prime}}(1)\cdots E_{i_{r}^{\prime}}(1)D(t_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,t_{r}^{\prime})E_{i_{r+1}^{\prime}}(c_{i_{r+1}^{\prime}}^{\prime})\cdots E_{i_{2r}^{\prime}}(c_{i_{2r}^{\prime}}^{\prime})

(4.15)

\displaystyle\mapsto E_{i_{2r}^{\prime}}(c_{i_{2r}^{\prime}}^{\prime})E_{i_{1}^{\prime}}(1)\cdots E_{i_{r}^{\prime}}(1)D(t_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,t_{r}^{\prime})E_{i_{r+1}^{\prime}}(c_{i_{r+1}^{\prime}}^{\prime})\cdots E_{i_{2r-1}^{\prime}}(c_{i_{2r-1}^{\prime}}^{\prime}),

or equivalently, the birational map
C_{i_{2r},+}
is given by conjugation by
E_{i_{2r}^{\prime}}(c_{i_{2r}^{\prime}}^{\prime})
.

Next, we have the following analogue of Proposition
3.3
, which describes refactorization relations in conjugation quotient Coxeter double Bruhat cells in terms of cluster transformations:

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
(u,v)
be a pair of Coxeter elements of
W
,
\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{2r}),\mathbf{i}^{\prime}=(i_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,i_{2r}^{\prime})
be double reduced words for
(u,v)
that differ by swapping two adjacent indices
\ell
and
\ell+1
differing only by a sign, and
|i_{\ell}|=j
. Then the coordinates on
G^{u,v}/H
given by cluster variables differ by the cluster transformation at
-j
:
\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{i}}}
\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}}
G^{u,v}/H
\mu_{-j}
\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}
\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}

Proof.

Firstly, we will show that if

(4.16)

D(t_{1},\ldots,t_{r})E_{i_{1}}(a_{i_{1}})\cdots E_{i_{2r}}(a_{i_{2r}})=D(t_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,t_{r}^{\prime})E_{i_{1}^{\prime}}(a_{i_{1}^{\prime}}^{\prime})\cdots E_{i_{2r}^{\prime}}(a_{i_{2r}^{\prime}}^{\prime}),

then we have

(4.17)

\displaystyle t_{i}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=t_{i}(1+\overline{a}_{j})^{\delta_{i,j}\varepsilon_{j}},

(4.18)

\displaystyle\overline{a}_{i}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\overline{a}_{i}(1+\overline{a}_{j})^{-\varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{j}C_{j,i}\delta_{i_{-}<j_{-}<i_{+}}}

for all
i\in[1,r]
. To this end, we first recall the following refactorization relations
[
FZ02b
]
:

E_{i}(t^{\prime})E_{-i}(t)=(1+tt^{\prime})^{\alpha_{i}^{\vee}}E_{-i}\left(t^{\prime}(1+tt^{\prime})\right)E_{i}\left(\frac{t}{1+tt^{\prime}}\right),\quad i\in[1,r].

Using the above refactorization relation, we have the following relation in
G^{u,v}/H
:

\displaystyle\quad D(t_{1},\ldots,t_{r})E_{i_{1}}(a_{i_{1}})\cdots E_{i_{\ell}}(a_{j_{-}})E_{i_{\ell+1}}(a_{j_{+}})\cdots E_{i_{2r}}(a_{i_{2r}})

(4.19)

\displaystyle=D(t_{1},\ldots,t_{r})E_{i_{1}}(a_{i_{1}})\cdots[(1+\overline{a}_{j})^{\varepsilon_{j}}]^{\alpha_{j}^{\vee}}E_{i_{\ell+1}}(a_{j_{+}}(1+\overline{a}_{j}))E_{i_{\ell}}(a_{j_{-}}/(1+\overline{a}_{j}))\cdots E_{i_{2r}}(a_{i_{2r}}).

The equations (
4.17
) and (
4.18
) now follow by equating the RHS of both (
4.16
) and (
4.19
), using the commutation relations
t^{\alpha_{i}^{\vee}}E_{\pm k}(a)=E_{\pm k}(at^{\pm C_{i,k}})t^{\alpha_{i}^{\vee}}
where necessary.

Next, we need to show that

(4.20)

\displaystyle(\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}\circ\mu_{-j})^{*}(t_{i}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}^{*}(t_{i}^{\prime}),\quad\text{and}

(4.21)

\displaystyle(\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}\circ\mu_{-j})^{*}(\overline{a}_{i}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}^{*}(\overline{a}_{i}^{\prime}),

for all
i\in[1,r]
. As we have
\varepsilon_{i}^{\prime}=\varepsilon_{i}
and
\mu_{-j}^{*}(\widetilde{A}_{\pm i}^{\prime})=\widetilde{A}_{\pm i}
for all
i\neq j
, it follows that (
4.20
) holds for all
i\in[1,r]\setminus\{j\}
. Similarly, as we have
\varepsilon_{j}^{\prime}\widetilde{B}_{k,-j}^{\prime}=\varepsilon_{j}\widetilde{B}_{k,-j}
and
\varepsilon_{j}^{\prime}\widetilde{B}_{-k,-j}^{\prime}=\varepsilon_{j}\widetilde{B}_{-k,-j}
for all
k\in[1,r]
, and
\widetilde{B}_{-k,-k}=0=\widetilde{B}_{-k,-k}^{\prime}
, it follows that (
4.21
) holds for
i=j
.

Next, we observe from equations (
3.4
) and (
3.6
) that we have

(4.22)

\displaystyle\widetilde{B}_{-i,-j}

\displaystyle=C_{i,j}\varepsilon_{j}\delta_{i_{-}<j_{-}<i_{+}},

(4.23)

\displaystyle\widetilde{B}_{i,-j}

\displaystyle=C_{i,j}\varepsilon_{j}(1-\delta_{i_{-}<j_{-}<i_{+}}),

(4.24)

\displaystyle\widetilde{B}_{-j,-i}

\displaystyle=-C_{j,i}\varepsilon_{j}\delta_{i_{-}<j_{-}<i_{+}},

(4.25)

\displaystyle\widetilde{B}_{j,-i}^{\prime}-\widetilde{B}_{j,-i}

\displaystyle=2\widetilde{B}_{-j,-i}

for all
i\neq j
. In particular, it follows from (
4.22
) and (
4.23
) that
\sgn(\widetilde{B}_{j,-j})\sgn(\widetilde{B}_{\pm i,-j})\leq 0
for all
i\neq j
, from which we deduce that

(4.26)

\mu_{-j}^{*}(\widetilde{A}_{-j}^{\prime})=\widetilde{A}_{-j}^{-1}\widetilde{A}_{j}^{2}\left(1+\prod_{i\in\widetilde{I}}\widetilde{A}_{i}^{-\varepsilon_{j}\widetilde{B}_{i,-j}}\right).

By (
3.15
) and (
4.26
), we have

\displaystyle(\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}\circ\mu_{-j})^{*}(t_{j}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\mu_{-j}^{*}(\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}^{*}(t_{j}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\mu_{-j}^{*}(\widetilde{A}_{j}^{\prime\varepsilon_{j}^{\prime}}\widetilde{A}_{-j}^{\prime-\varepsilon_{j}^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=\mu_{-j}^{*}(\widetilde{A}_{j}^{\prime-\varepsilon_{j}}\widetilde{A}_{-j}^{\prime\varepsilon_{j}})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{A}_{j}^{\varepsilon_{j}}\widetilde{A}_{-j}^{-\varepsilon_{j}}\left(1+\prod_{i\in\widetilde{I}}\widetilde{A}_{i}^{-\varepsilon_{j}\widetilde{B}_{i,-j}}\right)^{\varepsilon_{j}}.

On the other hand, it follows from (
3.15
) and (
4.17
) that we have

\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}^{*}(t_{j}^{\prime})=\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}^{*}(t_{j}(1+\overline{a}_{j})^{\varepsilon_{j}})=\widetilde{A}_{j}^{\varepsilon_{j}}\widetilde{A}_{-j}^{-\varepsilon_{j}}\left(1+\prod_{i\in\widetilde{I}}\widetilde{A}_{i}^{-\varepsilon_{j}\widetilde{B}_{i,-j}}\right)^{\varepsilon_{j}},

from which we deduce that (
4.20
) holds for
i=j
.

Finally, for all
i,k\neq j
, we have
\widetilde{B}_{k,-i}^{\prime}=\widetilde{B}_{k,-i}
and
\widetilde{B}_{-k,-i}^{\prime}=\widetilde{B}_{-k,-i}
. Thus, it follows from (
3.16
) and (
4.24
)-(
4.26
) that for all
i\neq j
, we have

\displaystyle(\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}\circ\mu_{-j})^{*}(\overline{a}_{i}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\mu_{-j}^{*}(\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}^{*}(\overline{a}_{i}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\mu_{-j}^{*}\left(\prod_{k\in\widetilde{I}}\widetilde{A}_{k}^{\prime-\varepsilon_{i}^{\prime}\widetilde{B}_{k,-i}^{\prime}}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(1+\prod_{m\in\widetilde{I}}\widetilde{A}_{m}^{-\varepsilon_{j}\widetilde{B}_{m,-j}}\right)^{-\varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{j}C_{j,i}\delta_{i_{-}<j_{-}<i_{+}}}\prod_{k\in\widetilde{I}}^{r}\widetilde{A}_{k}^{-\varepsilon_{i}\widetilde{B}_{k,-i}}.

On the other hand, it follows from (
3.16
) and (
4.21
) that we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}^{*}(\overline{a}_{i}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}^{*}(\overline{a}_{i}(1+\overline{a}_{j})^{-\varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{j}C_{j,i}\delta_{i_{-}<j_{-}<i_{+}}})

\displaystyle=\left(1+\prod_{m\in\widetilde{I}}\widetilde{A}_{m}^{-\varepsilon_{j}\widetilde{B}_{m,-j}}\right)^{-\varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{j}C_{j,i}\delta_{i_{-}<j_{-}<i_{+}}}\prod_{k\in\widetilde{I}}\widetilde{A}_{k}^{-\varepsilon_{i}\widetilde{B}_{k,-i}}

for all
i\in[1,r]\setminus\{j\}
. So this shows that (
4.21
) holds for all
i\in[1,r]\setminus\{j\}
, and we are done.
∎

We are now ready to construct the generalized Bäcklund-Darboux transformations between
G^{u,v}/H
and
G^{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}/H
in the case where
(u,v),(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})
satisfy the hypotheses of Lemma
3.10
. To begin, let us first consider the case where
(u,v),(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})
satisfy the first case of the hypotheses of Lemma
3.10
. Then there exists an unmixed double reduced word
\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{2r})
for
(u,v)
, such that
i_{1}=-k
. This implies that
\overline{\mathbf{i}}^{\prime}=(i_{2},\ldots,i_{2r},i_{1})
and
\mathbf{i}^{\prime}=(i_{2},\ldots,i_{r},i_{1},i_{r+1},\ldots,i_{2r})
are double reduced words for
(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})
, with
\mathbf{i}^{\prime}
being unmixed. Thus, we have a map
\rho_{\tau_{k}}:G^{u,v}/H\to G^{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}/H
, given in terms of the composition of the conjugation map
C_{-k,-}:G^{u,v}/H\to G^{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}/H
given in (
4.14
), and the refactorization map
G^{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}/H\to G^{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}/H
given in (
4.19
), as follows:

\displaystyle\quad E_{i_{1}}(1)\cdots E_{i_{r}}(1)D(t_{1},\ldots,t_{r})E_{i_{r+1}}(c_{i_{r+1}})\cdots E_{i_{2r}}(c_{i_{2r}})

\displaystyle\mapsto E_{i_{2}}(1)\cdots E_{i_{r}}(1)D(t_{1},\ldots,t_{r})E_{i_{r+1}}(c_{i_{r+1}})\cdots E_{i_{2r}}(c_{i_{2r}})E_{i_{1}}(1)

(4.27)

\displaystyle=E_{i_{2}}(1)\cdots E_{i_{r}}(1)E_{i_{1}}(1)D(t_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,t_{r}^{\prime})E_{i_{r+1}}(c_{i_{r+1}}^{\prime})\cdots E_{i_{2r}}(c_{i_{2r}}^{\prime})

As the map
C_{-k,-}
is Poisson, it implies that the map
\rho_{\tau_{k}}
is also Poisson as well, and the maps
\tau_{k}=\mu_{-k}\circ\sigma_{k}:\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{(u,v)}}\to\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})}}
and
\rho_{\tau_{k}}:G^{u,v}/H\to G^{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}/H
intertwine the maps
\widetilde{a}_{(u,v)}:\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{(u,v)}}\to G^{u,v}/H
and
\widetilde{a}_{(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})}:\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})}}\to G^{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}/H
, that is, we have
\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{(u,v)}}
G^{u,v}/H
\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})}}
G^{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}/H
\widetilde{a}_{(u,v)}
\rho_{\tau_{k}}
\tau_{k}
\widetilde{a}_{(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})}

Moreover, we can check that the factorization parameters
c_{1},\ldots,c_{r},t_{1},\ldots,t_{r}
on
G^{u,v}/H
and the factorization parameters
c_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,c_{r}^{\prime},t_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,t_{r}^{\prime}
on
G^{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}/H
are related to each other as follows:

(4.28)

\displaystyle\rho_{\tau_{k}}^{*}(t_{i}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=t_{i}(1+c_{k})^{\delta_{i,k}},

(4.29)

\displaystyle\rho_{\tau_{k}}^{*}(c_{i}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\frac{c_{k}}{(1+c_{k})^{2}}\prod_{j=1}^{r}t_{j}^{-C_{j,k}}&\text{if }i=k,\\
c_{i}(1+c_{k})^{-C_{k,i}\delta_{i_{+}<k_{+}}}&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

(4.30)

\displaystyle(\rho_{\tau_{k}}^{-1})^{*}(t_{i})

\displaystyle=t_{i}^{\prime}\left(1+c_{k}^{\prime}\prod_{j=1}^{r}t_{j}^{\prime C_{j,k}}\right)^{-\delta_{i,k}},

(4.31)

\displaystyle(\rho_{\tau_{k}}^{-1})^{*}(c_{i})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}c_{k}^{\prime}\prod_{j=1}^{r}t_{j}^{\prime C_{j,k}}&\text{if }i=k,\\
c_{i}^{\prime}\left(1+c_{k}^{\prime}\prod_{j=1}^{r}t_{j}^{\prime C_{j,k}}\right)^{C_{k,i}\delta_{i_{+}<k_{+}}}&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

When
(u,v),(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})
satisfy the second case of the hypotheses of Lemma
3.10
, the birational Poisson map
\rho_{\tau_{k}}:G^{u,v}/H\to G^{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}/H
and its inverse
\rho_{\tau_{k}}^{-1}:G^{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}/H\to G^{u,v}/H
are defined in a similar manner as well. We will omit the details here.

Following
[
GSV11
]
, we call these maps
\rho_{\tau_{k}}
and
\rho_{\tau_{k}}^{-1}
elementary generalized Bäcklund-Darboux transformations. The partial rationale behind such a term comes from the fact that the map
C_{-k,-}
interchanges the factors
E_{i_{1}}(1),\ldots,E_{i_{r}}(1),E_{i_{r+1}}(c_{i_{r+1}}),\ldots,E_{i_{2r}}(c_{i_{2r}}),

D(t_{1},\ldots,t_{r})
via conjugation. However, as in
[
GSV11
]
, the rearrangement of factors is followed by a refactorization relation, and so we have a transformation of the factorization parameters
c_{1},\ldots,c_{r},t_{1},\ldots,t_{r}
. Later, we will show that these transformations preserve Hamiltonian flows generated by Coxeter-Toda Hamiltonians arising from trace functions of representations of
G
.

Remark
.

Here, we remark that in the case where
G=SL_{r+1}(\mathbb{C})
and
k\neq 1
, these formulas are essentially the same as that obtained in
[
GSV11
, Section 6.1]
.

For the general case, we let
(u,v),(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})
be any pairs of Coxeter elements. By Lemma
3.10
and Corollary
3.11
, there exist
j_{1},\ldots,j_{m}\in[1,r]
and
n_{1},\ldots,n_{m}\in\{\pm 1\}
, such that

\widetilde{\Sigma}_{(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})}=(\tau_{j_{m}}^{n_{m}}\circ\cdots\circ\tau_{j_{1}}^{n_{1}})(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{(u,v)}).

We let
\tau=\tau_{j_{m}}^{n_{m}}\circ\cdots\circ\tau_{j_{1}}^{n_{1}}
and
\rho_{\tau}=\rho_{\tau_{j_{m}}}^{n_{m}}\circ\cdots\circ\rho_{\tau_{j_{1}}}^{n_{1}}
. Then it follows that the map
\tau:\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{(u,v)}}\to\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})}}
and the birational Poisson map
\rho_{\tau}:G^{u,v}/H\to G^{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}/H
intertwine the maps
\widetilde{a}_{(u,v)}:\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{(u,v)}}\to G^{u,v}/H
and
\widetilde{a}_{(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})}:\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})}}\to G^{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}/H
, that is, we have:
\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{(u,v)}}
G^{u,v}/H
\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})}}
G^{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}/H
\widetilde{a}_{(u,v)}
\rho_{\tau}
\tau
\widetilde{a}_{(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})}

Following
[
GSV11
]
, we call
\rho_{\tau}
a generalized Bäcklund-Darboux transformation from
G^{u,v}/H
to
G^{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}/H
.

Our next goal is to show that these generalized Bäcklund-Darboux transformations preserve Hamiltonian flows generated by Coxeter-Toda Hamiltonians arising from trace functions of representations of
G
. Before we describe our result further, we would like to recall the results proved in
[
GSV11
]
for the case
G=SL_{r+1}(\mathbb{C})
.

For each
j=1,\ldots,r
,
X\in G
and a pair of Coxeter elements
(u,v)
, we let
\tilde{H}_{j}(X)=\tr(X^{j})
, and
\tilde{H}_{j}^{u,v}
denote the restriction of the Hamiltonian
\tilde{H}_{j}
to
G^{u,v}/H
. Then with respect to any Coxeter-Toda system on
G^{u,v}/H
, it follows that
\{\tilde{H}_{1}^{u,v},\ldots,\tilde{H}_{r}^{u,v}\}
is also a full set of algebraically independent integrals of motion
[
Kos79
]
. Gekhtman
et al
.
[
GSV11
]
showed that the generalized Bäcklund-Darboux transformation
\rho_{\tau}:G^{u,v}/H\to G^{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}/H
sends the Hamiltonian flow generated by
\tilde{H}_{j}^{u,v}
on
G^{u,v}/H
to the Hamiltonian flow generated by
\tilde{H}_{j}^{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}
on
G^{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}/H
for all
j=1,\ldots,r
, that is, if
c_{i}(t)
and
t_{i}(t)
satisfy

(4.32)

\frac{dc_{i}}{dt}=\left\{c_{i},\tilde{H}_{j}^{u,v}\right\}_{u,v},\quad\frac{dt_{i}}{dt}=\left\{t_{i},\tilde{H}_{j}^{u,v}\right\}_{u,v},

then
c_{i}^{\prime}(t)
and
t_{i}^{\prime}(t)
satisfy

(4.33)

\frac{d(c_{i}^{\prime}\circ\rho_{\tau})}{dt}=\left\{c_{i}^{\prime},\tilde{H}_{j}^{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}\right\}_{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}\circ\rho_{\tau},\quad\frac{d(t_{i}^{\prime}\circ\rho_{\tau})}{dt}=\left\{t_{i}^{\prime},\tilde{H}_{j}^{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}\right\}_{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}\circ\rho_{\tau}.

In particular, as
H_{1},\ldots,H_{r}
and
\tilde{H}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{H}_{r}
can be obtained from each other via the Faddeev–LeVerrier algorithm, it follows that if
c_{i}(t)
and
t_{i}(t)
satisfy

(4.34)

\frac{dc_{i}}{dt}=\left\{c_{i},H_{j}^{u,v}\right\}_{u,v},\quad\frac{dt_{i}}{dt}=\left\{t_{i},H_{j}^{u,v}\right\}_{u,v},

then
c_{i}^{\prime}(t)
and
t_{i}^{\prime}(t)
satisfy

(4.35)

\frac{d(c_{i}^{\prime}\circ\rho_{\tau})}{dt}=\left\{c_{i}^{\prime},H_{j}^{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}\right\}_{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}\circ\rho_{\tau},\quad\frac{d(t_{i}^{\prime}\circ\rho_{\tau})}{dt}=\left\{t_{i}^{\prime},H_{j}^{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}\right\}_{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}\circ\rho_{\tau}.

Their proof of the statement essentially follows from the following ingredients:

(1)

Gekhtman
et al
.
[
GSV11
]
showed that if an element
X
in
G^{u,v}/H
admits the factorization scheme (
2.3
), then
X
can be recovered from its Weyl function
m(\lambda;X)=(\lambda I-X)^{-1}_{1,1}=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}h_{i}(X)\lambda^{i}
via Hankel determinants,

(2)

If
\rho_{\tau}=\rho_{\tau_{k_{n}}}\circ\cdots\circ\rho_{\tau_{k_{1}}}:G^{u,v}/H\to G^{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}/H
is a generalized Bäcklund-Darboux transformation with
|k_{1}|,\ldots,|k_{n}|\neq 1
, and
X
is a generic element in
G^{u,v}/H
with
X^{\prime}=\rho_{\tau}(X)
, then
m(\lambda;X)=m(\lambda;X^{\prime})
, and

(3)

For any
j=1,\ldots,r
, the Hamiltonian equations obtained from the Hamiltonian flow generated by
\tilde{H}_{j}^{u,v}
on
G^{u,v}/H
is equivalent to the following evolution of the coefficients of the Laurent expansion of the Weyl function of
X
:

(4.36)

\frac{d}{dt}h_{i}(X)=h_{i+j}(X)-h_{i}(X)h_{j}(X).

As we have
m(\lambda;X)=\frac{q(\lambda)}{p(\lambda)}
, where
p(\lambda)
is the characteristic polynomial of
X
and
q(\lambda)
is the characteristic polynomial of the
r\times r
submatrix of
X
formed by deleting the first row and first column of
X
, and the non-trivial coefficients of
p(\lambda)
are precisely the Hamiltonians
H_{1}^{u,v}(X),\ldots,H_{r}^{u,v}(X)
, we have
H_{j}^{u,v}=H_{j}^{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}\circ\rho_{\tau}
for all
j=1,\ldots,r
.

We have the following generalization of the above result obtained in
[
GSV11
]
to the other finite Dynkin types:

Theorem 4.3
.

Let
(u,v)
and
(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})
be two pairs of Coxeter elements, and
\rho_{\tau}:G^{u,v}/H\to G^{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}/H
be a generalized Bäcklund-Darboux transformation. Let
\pi_{V}:G\to SL(V)
be a finite-dimensional representation of
G
, and for all
X\in G
, we define
H_{V}(X)=\tr(\pi_{V}(X))
. Then the birational Poisson map
\rho_{\tau}:G^{u,v}/H\to G^{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}/H
sends the Hamiltonian flow generated by the Coxeter-Toda Hamiltonian
H_{V}^{u,v}
on
G^{u,v}/H
to the Hamiltonian flow generated by the Coxeter-Toda Hamiltonian
H_{V}^{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}
on
G^{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}/H
. More precisely, if
c_{i}(t),t_{i}(t)
satisfy

\frac{dc_{i}}{dt}=\left\{c_{i},H_{V}^{u,v}\right\}_{u,v},\quad\frac{dt_{i}}{dt}=\left\{t_{i},H_{V}^{u,v}\right\}_{u,v},

for all
i=1,\ldots,r
, then we have

\frac{d(c_{i}^{\prime}\circ\rho_{\tau})}{dt}=\left\{c_{i}^{\prime},H_{V}^{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}\right\}_{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}\circ\rho_{\tau},\quad\frac{d(t_{i}^{\prime}\circ\rho_{\tau})}{dt}=\left\{t_{i}^{\prime},H_{V}^{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}\right\}_{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}\circ\rho_{\tau}.

In particular, Theorem
4.3
implies that the generalized Bäcklund-Darboux transformation
\rho_{\tau}:G^{u,v}/H\to G^{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}/H
preserves the Coxeter-Toda systems on both Coxeter double Bruhat cells
G^{u,v}/H
and
G^{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}/H
. As
\rho_{\tau}
is induced from the map
\tau:\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{(u,v)}}\to\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})}}
, which itself is a composition of cluster transformations and permutations of cluster seeds, this shows that the family of Coxeter-Toda systems on
G
forms a single cluster integrable system.

Instead of following the approach using Weyl functions employed by Gekhtman
et al
.
[
GSV11
]
, we will prove Theorem
4.3
in a different manner using the following theorem:

Theorem 4.4
.

Let
(u,v)
and
(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})
be pairs of Coxeter elements, and
\rho_{\tau}:G^{u,v}/H\to G^{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}/H
be a generalized Bäcklund-Darboux transformation. Let
\pi_{V}:G\to SL(V)
be a finite-dimensional representation of
G
, and
H_{V}=\tr\circ\pi_{V}:G\to\mathbb{C}
be the trace function of
V
. Then we have

H_{V}^{u,v}=H_{V}^{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}\circ\rho_{\tau}.

Proof.

Let us write
\tau=\tau_{j_{m}}^{n_{m}}\circ\cdots\circ\tau_{j_{1}}^{n_{1}}
for some
j_{1},\ldots,j_{m}\in[1,r]
and
n_{1},\ldots,n_{m}\in\{\pm 1\}
. As each
\rho_{\tau_{j_{i}}}
is the composition of a conjugation map followed by a refactorization relation, it follows that the trace function
H_{V}
is preserved under the map
\rho_{\tau_{j_{i}}}^{n_{i}}
for all
i\in[1,m]
, from which the desired statement holds.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.3
.

By equations (
4.28
)-(
4.31
), it is easy to check using the chain rule on composite functions and the Leibniz rule satisfied by the Poisson bracket
\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{u,v}
that we have

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}(t_{i}^{\prime}\circ\rho_{\tau})

\displaystyle=\{t_{i}^{\prime}\circ\rho_{\tau},H_{V}^{u,v}\}_{u,v},\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}(c_{i}^{\prime}\circ\rho_{\tau})

\displaystyle=\{c_{i}^{\prime}\circ\rho_{\tau},H_{V}^{u,v}\}_{u,v}

By Theorem
4.4
, along with the fact that the map
\rho_{\tau}
is Poisson, we have

\frac{d}{dt}(t_{i}^{\prime}\circ\rho_{\tau})=\{t_{i}^{\prime}\circ\rho_{\tau},H_{V}^{u,v}\}_{u,v}=\{t_{i}^{\prime}\circ\rho_{\tau},H_{V}^{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}\circ\rho_{\tau}\}_{u,v}=\{t_{i}^{\prime},H_{V}^{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}\}_{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}\circ\rho_{\tau}.

By a similar argument as above, we have

\frac{d}{dt}(c_{i}^{\prime}\circ\rho_{\tau})=\{c_{i}^{\prime},H_{V}^{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}\}_{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}\circ\rho_{\tau},

and this completes the proof.
∎

4.2.
The factorization mapping in terms of generalized Bäcklund-Darboux transformations

Throughout this subsection, we shall fix a pair
(u,v)
of Coxeter elements, and an unmixed double reduced word
\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{2r})
for
(u,v)
. It was shown in
[
GSV11
, Section 6.2]
that the following factorization mapping

\displaystyle\quad E_{i_{1}}(1)\cdots E_{i_{r}}(1)D(t_{1},\ldots,t_{r})E_{i_{r+1}}(c_{i_{r+1}})\cdots E_{i_{2r}}(c_{i_{2r}})

\displaystyle\mapsto D(t_{1},\ldots,t_{r})E_{i_{r+1}}(c_{i_{r+1}})\cdots E_{i_{2r}}(c_{i_{2r}})E_{i_{1}}(1)\cdots E_{i_{r}}(1)

(4.37)

\displaystyle=E_{i_{1}}(1)\cdots E_{i_{r}}(1)D(t_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,t_{r}^{\prime})E_{i_{r+1}}(c_{i_{r+1}}^{\prime})\cdots E_{i_{2r}}(c_{i_{2r}}^{\prime})

can be described in terms of elementary generalized Bäcklund-Darboux transformations in the case where
G=SL_{r+1}(\mathbb{C})
. Our goal in this subsection is to describe the factorization mapping (
4.37
) in general:

Theorem 4.5
.

The factorization mapping (
4.37
) coincides with the generalized Bäcklund-Darboux transformation
\rho_{\tau}:G^{u,v}/H\to G^{u,v}/H
, where
\tau=\tau_{|i_{r}|}\circ\cdots\circ\tau_{|i_{1}|}
.

In order to prove Theorem
4.5
, we need a few auxiliary lemmas:

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
(w_{1},w_{2})
be a pair of Coxeter elements, and
\mathbf{j}=(j_{1},\ldots,j_{2r})
be a double reduced word for
(w_{1},w_{2})
. Then for any distinct
i,j\in[1,r]
, we have
(\mu_{-j}\circ\sigma_{i})(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{j}})=(\sigma_{i}\circ\mu_{-j})(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{j}})
, that is, the cluster mutation
\mu_{-j}
commutes with the permutation
\sigma_{i}
.

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
\mathbf{i}^{\prime}=(i_{r+1},\ldots,i_{2r},i_{1},\ldots,i_{r})
. Then
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}=\sigma(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{i}})
, where
\sigma
is the permutation of
\widetilde{I}
given by
\sigma(i)=-i
for all
i\in\widetilde{I}
.

Proof.

By the definition of
\mathbf{i}^{\prime}
, it follows that we have
\varepsilon_{j}^{\prime}=-\varepsilon_{j}=1
,
j_{-}^{\prime}=j_{+}-r
and
j_{+}^{\prime}=j_{-}+r
. Using (
3.4
)-(
3.10
), it follows that we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{B}_{-j,-k}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{C_{j,k}}{2}[(\delta_{j_{-}^{\prime}<k_{-}^{\prime}<j_{+}^{\prime}}+\delta_{j_{-}^{\prime}<k_{+}^{\prime}<j_{+}^{\prime}})-(\delta_{k_{-}^{\prime}<j_{-}^{\prime}<k_{+}^{\prime}}+\delta_{k_{-}^{\prime}<j_{+}^{\prime}<k_{+}^{\prime}})]

\displaystyle=\frac{C_{j,k}}{2}(\delta_{j_{+}<k_{+}}+\delta_{k_{-}<j_{-}}-\delta_{k_{+}<j_{+}}-\delta_{j_{-}<k_{-}})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{B}_{j,k},

\displaystyle\widetilde{B}_{-j,k}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=-\frac{C_{j,k}}{2}[2\delta_{j,k}+\delta_{j_{-}^{\prime}<k_{-}^{\prime}}+\delta_{j_{+}^{\prime}<k_{-}^{\prime}}+\delta_{k_{+}^{\prime}<j_{-}^{\prime}}+\delta_{k_{+}^{\prime}<j_{+}^{\prime}}+\delta_{j_{-}^{\prime}<k_{-}^{\prime}<j_{+}^{\prime}}+\delta_{j_{-}^{\prime}<k_{+}^{\prime}<j_{+}^{\prime}}]

\displaystyle=-C_{j,k}(\delta_{j,k}+\delta_{j_{+}<k_{+}}+\delta_{k_{-}<j_{-}})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{B}_{j,-k},

\displaystyle\widetilde{B}_{j,-k}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{C_{j,k}}{2}[2\delta_{j,k}+\delta_{k_{-}^{\prime}<j_{-}^{\prime}}+\delta_{k_{+}^{\prime}<j_{-}^{\prime}}+\delta_{j_{+}^{\prime}<k_{-}^{\prime}}+\delta_{j_{+}^{\prime}<k_{+}^{\prime}}+\delta_{k_{-}^{\prime}<j_{-}^{\prime}<k_{+}^{\prime}}+\delta_{k_{-}^{\prime}<j_{+}^{\prime}<k_{+}^{\prime}}]

\displaystyle=C_{j,k}(\delta_{j,k}+\delta_{j_{-}<k_{-}}+\delta_{k_{+}<j_{+}})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{B}_{-j,k},

\displaystyle\widetilde{B}_{j,k}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{C_{j,k}}{2}[(\delta_{j_{-}^{\prime}<k_{-}^{\prime}}+\delta_{j_{+}^{\prime}<k_{-}^{\prime}}+\delta_{k_{+}^{\prime}<j_{-}^{\prime}}+\delta_{k_{+}^{\prime}<j_{+}^{\prime}})-(\delta_{k_{-}^{\prime}<j_{-}^{\prime}}+\delta_{k_{+}^{\prime}<j_{-}^{\prime}}+\delta_{j_{+}^{\prime}<k_{-}^{\prime}}+\delta_{j_{+}^{\prime}<k_{+}^{\prime}})]

\displaystyle=\frac{C_{j,k}}{2}(\delta_{j_{+}<k_{+}}+\delta_{k_{-}<j_{-}}-\delta_{k_{+}<j_{+}}-\delta_{j_{-}<k_{-}})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{B}_{-j,-k}

for all
j,k\in[1,r]
. This completes the proof of Lemma
4.7
.

∎

Proposition 4.8
.

Let
\mathbf{i}^{\prime}=(i_{r+1},\ldots,i_{2r},i_{1},\ldots,i_{r})
, and
\sigma
be the permutation of
\widetilde{I}
given by
\sigma(i)=-i
for all
i\in\widetilde{I}
. Then the map
\sigma:\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{i}}}\to\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}}
induces a map
f_{\sigma}:G^{u,v}/H\to G^{u,v}/H
, given by

\displaystyle\quad E_{i_{1}}(1)\cdots E_{i_{r}}(1)D(t_{1},\ldots,t_{r})E_{i_{r+1}}(c_{i_{r+1}})\cdots E_{i_{2r}}(c_{i_{2r}})

(4.38)

\displaystyle\mapsto D(t_{1},\ldots,t_{r})E_{i_{r+1}}(c_{i_{r+1}})\cdots E_{i_{2r}}(c_{i_{2r}})E_{i_{1}}(1)\cdots E_{i_{r}}(1),

such that the maps
\sigma
and
f_{\tau}
intertwine the maps
\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}:\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{i}}}\to G^{u,v}/H
and
\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}:\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}}\to G^{u,v}/H
, that is, we have
\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{i}}}
\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}}
G^{u,v}/H
G^{u,v}/H
\sigma
\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}
\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}
f_{\sigma}

Proof.

Firstly, we have
\overline{a}_{j}^{\prime}=c_{j}
for all
j\in[1,r]
, which implies that we have

(4.39)

f_{\sigma}^{*}(\overline{a}_{j}^{\prime})=\overline{a}_{j}\prod_{i=1}^{r}t_{i}^{-C_{i,j}},\quad f_{\sigma}^{*}(t_{j}^{\prime})=t_{j}.

Let us show that

(4.40)

\displaystyle(\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}\circ\sigma)^{*}(t_{j}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(f_{\sigma}\circ\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}})^{*}(t_{j}^{\prime}),\quad\text{and}

(4.41)

\displaystyle(\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}\circ\sigma)^{*}(\overline{a}_{j}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(f_{\sigma}\circ\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}})^{*}(\overline{a}_{j}^{\prime}),

for all
j\in[1,r]
. Firstly, by Lemma
4.7
, (
3.15
) and (
4.39
), we have

\displaystyle(\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}\circ\sigma)^{*}(t_{j}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sigma^{*}(\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}^{*}(t_{j}^{\prime}))=\sigma^{*}(\widetilde{A}_{j}^{\prime}\widetilde{A}_{-j}^{\prime-1})=\widetilde{A}_{-j}\widetilde{A}_{j}^{-1},

\displaystyle(f_{\sigma}\circ\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}})^{*}(t_{j}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}^{*}(f_{\sigma}^{*}(t_{j}^{\prime}))=\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}^{*}(t_{j})=\widetilde{A}_{j}^{-1}\widetilde{A}_{-j},

from which we see that (
4.40
) holds. Next, by Lemma
4.7
, (
3.15
), (
3.16
) and (
4.39
), we have

\displaystyle(\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}\circ\sigma)^{*}(\overline{a}_{j}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sigma^{*}(\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}^{*}(\overline{a}_{j}^{\prime}))=\sigma^{*}\left(\prod_{k\in\widetilde{I}}\widetilde{A}_{k}^{\prime-\widetilde{B}_{k,-j}^{\prime}}\right)=\prod_{k\in\widetilde{I}}\widetilde{A}_{-k}^{-\widetilde{B}_{-k,j}}=\prod_{k\in\widetilde{I}}\widetilde{A}_{k}^{-\widetilde{B}_{k,j}},

\displaystyle(f_{\sigma}\circ\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}})^{*}(\overline{a}_{j}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}^{*}(f_{\sigma}^{*}(\overline{a}_{j}^{\prime}))=\widetilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}}^{*}\left(\overline{a}_{j}\prod_{i=1}^{r}t_{i}^{-C_{i,j}}\right)=\prod_{k\in\widetilde{I}}\widetilde{A}_{k}^{\widetilde{B}_{k,-j}}\prod_{i=1}^{r}\widetilde{A}_{i}^{C_{i,j}}\widetilde{A}_{-i}^{-C_{i,j}}=\prod_{k\in\widetilde{I}}\widetilde{A}_{k}^{\widetilde{B}_{k,j}},

where the last equality follows from the fact that we have
-\widetilde{B}_{k,j}=\widetilde{B}_{k,-j}+C_{k,j}
and
-\widetilde{B}_{-k,j}=\widetilde{B}_{-k,-j}-C_{k,j}
for all
j,k\in[1,r]
. So this shows that (
4.41
) holds, and we are done.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.5
.

Firstly, by Lemma
4.6
, we have

(4.42)

\tau=\tau_{|i_{r}|}\circ\cdots\circ\tau_{|i_{1}|}=\mu_{-|i_{r}|}\circ\sigma_{|i_{r}|}\circ\cdots\circ\mu_{-|i_{1}|}\circ\sigma_{|i_{1}|}=\mu_{-|i_{r}|}\circ\cdots\circ\mu_{-|i_{1}|}\circ\sigma,

where
\sigma
is the permutation of
\widetilde{I}
given by
\sigma(i)=-i
for all
i\in\widetilde{I}
. Next, by Proposition
4.8
, the map
\sigma:\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{i}}}\to\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}}
induces a map
f_{\sigma}:G^{u,v}/H\to G^{u,v}/H
, given by (
4.38
). Finally, by Propositions
3.6
and
4.2
, the composition of cluster transformations
\mu=\mu_{-|i_{r}|}\circ\cdots\circ\mu_{-|i_{1}|}
yields a refactorization relation

\displaystyle\quad D(t_{1},\ldots,t_{r})E_{i_{r+1}}(c_{i_{r+1}})\cdots E_{i_{2r}}(c_{i_{2r}})E_{i_{1}}(1)\cdots E_{i_{r}}(1)

(4.43)

\displaystyle=E_{i_{1}}(1)\cdots E_{i_{r}}(1)D(t_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,t_{r}^{\prime})E_{i_{r+1}}(c_{i_{r+1}}^{\prime})\cdots E_{i_{2r}}(c_{i_{2r}}^{\prime}).

By composing the map
f_{\sigma}:G^{u,v}/H\to G^{u,v}/H
given by (
4.38
) along with the refactorization relation (
4.43
), we deduce from (
4.42
) that the factorization mapping given by (
4.37
) coincides with the generalized Bäcklund-Darboux transformation
\rho_{\tau}:G^{u,v}/H\to G^{u,v}/H
as desired.
∎

Our next step is to make an explicit connection between the factorization mapping on
G^{u,u}/H
given by (
4.38
), where
u
is any Coxeter element of
W
, and the normalized
Q
-system dynamics given by (
2.14
). Here, we remark that while such a connection has already been established in
[
GSV11
,
Wil15
]
, we will need an explicit formulation, as we would like to express our Coxeter-Toda Hamiltonians
f_{j}^{u,u}
and
H_{j}^{u,u}
,
j\in[1,r]
, in terms of the initial data
(R_{\alpha,0},R_{\alpha,1})_{\alpha\in[1,r]}
of normalized
Q
-system variables, in the case where
G
is of types
ABCD
, which would be the subject of interest in the next Section. While our notations and conventions will differ slightly from the notations used in
2.5
, the content will essentially be the same.

To begin, we first recall from (
3.8
)-(
3.10
) that the exchange matrix corresponding to the seed
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{(u,u)}
is given by

(4.44)

\widetilde{B}_{(u,u)}=\begin{pmatrix}0&C\\
-C&0\end{pmatrix},

where the row and column indices are ordered as
-1,\ldots,-r,1,\ldots,r
. Let us define
\widetilde{A}_{\pm i}^{(k)}=(\tau^{*})^{k}(\widetilde{A}_{\pm i})
for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}
and
i\in[1,r]
. Then by Theorem
2.10
, we have
A_{i}^{(m+1)}=A_{-i}^{(m)}
, and

(4.45)

A_{i}^{(m+1)}A_{i}^{(m-1)}=(A_{i}^{(m)})^{2}+\prod_{j\sim i}(A_{j}^{(m)})^{-C_{j,i}}

for all
m\in\mathbb{Z}
and
i\in[1,r]
. Hence, by (
2.14
) and (
4.45
), there is an identification of the cluster variables
A_{i}^{(m)}
with the normalized
Q
-system variables
R_{i,m}
, given by
A_{i}^{(m)}\mapsto R_{i,m}
, where the relationship between the finite Dynkin type
Y_{r}
of the simple complex Lie group
G
and the corresponding affine Dynkin type
X_{m}^{(\kappa)}
of the
Q
-system is given by the table at the start of Section
2.5
. With this identification, it follows from (
4.2
)-(
4.3
) and (
4.44
) that we have

(4.46)

\displaystyle\widetilde{a}_{(u,v)}^{*}(t_{j})

\displaystyle=R_{j,1}R_{j,0}^{-1},\quad\text{and}

(4.47)

\displaystyle\widetilde{a}_{(u,v)}^{*}(c_{j})

\displaystyle=\prod_{k=1}^{r}R_{k,1}^{-C_{k,j}}

for all
j\in[1,r]
.

Now, as the generalized Bäcklund-Darboux transformation
\rho_{\tau}:G^{u,u}/H\to G^{u,u}/H
(or equivalently, the factorization mapping (
4.37
)) coincides with the normalized
Q
-system evolution
R_{i,k}\mapsto R_{i,k+1}
, and the map
\rho_{\tau}:G^{u,u}/H\to G^{u,u}/H
preserves the Coxeter-Toda Hamiltonians
H_{j}^{u,u}
and
f_{j}^{u,u}
for all
j\in[1,r]
by Theorem
4.4
, it follows that the Coxeter-Toda Hamiltonians
H_{j}^{u,u}
and
f_{j}^{u,u}
pull back to conserved quantities
C_{j}=\rho_{\tau}^{*}(H_{j}^{u,u})
and
\widetilde{C}_{j}=\rho_{\tau}^{*}(f_{j}^{c,c})
for the normalized
Q
-system of type
X_{m}^{(\kappa)}
, in the sense that we have

(4.48)

\displaystyle\rho_{\tau}^{*}(H_{j}^{u,u})(R_{\alpha,k},R_{\alpha,k+1})_{\alpha\in[1,r]}

\displaystyle=\rho_{\tau}^{*}(H_{j}^{u,u})(R_{\alpha,k+1},R_{\alpha,k+2})_{\alpha\in[1,r]},

(4.49)

\displaystyle\rho_{\tau}^{*}(f_{j}^{u,u})(R_{\alpha,k},R_{\alpha,k+1})_{\alpha\in[1,r]}

\displaystyle=\rho_{\tau}^{*}(f_{j}^{u,u})(R_{\alpha,k+1},R_{\alpha,k+2})_{\alpha\in[1,r]},

or equivalently,

(4.50)

\displaystyle C_{j}(\mathbf{y}_{k})

\displaystyle=\rho_{\tau}^{*}(H_{j}^{u,u})(\mathbf{y}_{k})=\rho_{\tau}^{*}(H_{j}^{u,u})(\mathbf{y}_{k+1})=C_{j}(\mathbf{y}_{k+1}),

(4.51)

\displaystyle\widetilde{C}_{j}(\mathbf{y}_{k})

\displaystyle=\rho_{\tau}^{*}(f_{j}^{u,u})(\mathbf{y}_{k})=\rho_{\tau}^{*}(f_{j}^{u,u})(\mathbf{y}_{k+1})=\widetilde{C}_{j}(\mathbf{y}_{k+1})

for all
j\in[1,r]
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, where
\mathbf{y}_{k}
is the
k
-th fundamental initial data for the (un-normalized)
X_{m}^{(\kappa)}

Q
-system defined by (
2.13
).

5.
Conserved quantities of
Q
-systems

In this section, we will give combinatorial formulas for the Coxeter-Toda Hamiltonians arising from traces of the fundamental representations or the exterior powers of the defining representation of a simple classical complex Lie group
G
of rank
r
, or equivalently, combinatorial formulas for the conserved quantities of
Q
-systems of types
A_{r}^{(1)}
,
D_{r+1}^{(2)}
,
A_{2r-1}^{(2)}
and
D_{r}^{(1)}
. Here, we remark that while some of these formulas were previously given in
[
Res00
,
KM15
,
Wil16
]
, they were given in terms of functions of cluster
\mathcal{X}
-coordinates with fractional exponents. In what follows, the combinatorial formulas that we will provide for these Coxeter-Toda Hamiltonians in the subsequent subsections will be given in terms of regular functions of normalized
Q
-system variables via (
4.46
) and (
4.47
).

In order to arrive at these combinatorial formulas, we would need a network formulation of our Coxeter-Toda Hamiltonians
f_{k}^{u,u}
using the network formulations of elements of the quotient Coxeter double Bruhat cell
G^{u,u}/H
developed in Section
2.6
, where
u
is a Coxeter element of
W
, and apply the Lindström-Gessel-Viennot lemma to these networks, which we will explain in detail in Section
5.1
. While the combinatorial formulas for these Coxeter-Toda Hamiltonians is independent of the choice of the Coxeter element
u
, it will be more convenient for us to fix
u=c
, where
c=s_{1}s_{2}\cdots s_{r}
, along with the unmixed double reduced word
\mathbf{i}=(-1,\ldots,-r,1,\ldots,r)
for
(c,c)
throughout this section. In subsequent subsections, we will refine these network formulations further for each classical Dynkin type in an amenable manner that will allow us to deduce combinatorial formulas for the conserved quantities of
Q
-systems for the aforementioned affine Dynkin types.

5.1.
Network formulations of Coxeter-Toda Hamiltonians

In this subsection, we will give network formulations of the Coxeter-Toda Hamiltonians
f_{k}^{c,c}
. Using the fact that the Hamiltonians
H_{j}
can be expressed as linear combinations of
f_{0},f_{1},\ldots,f_{j}
as follows:

•

If
G=SL_{r+1}(\mathbb{C})
, then
H_{j}=f_{j}
for all
j=1,\ldots,r
,

•

If
G=SO_{2r+1}(\mathbb{C})
, then
H_{j}=f_{j}
for all
j=1,\ldots,r-1
,

•

If
G=Sp_{2r}(\mathbb{C})
, then
H_{2j}=\sum_{i=0}^{j}f_{2i}
and
H_{2\ell+1}=\sum_{i=0}^{\ell}f_{2i+1}
for all
j=1,\ldots,\left\lceil\frac{r-1}{2}\right\rceil
and
\ell=0,\ldots,\left\lfloor\frac{r-1}{2}\right\rfloor
,

•

If
G=SO_{2r}(\mathbb{C})
, then
H_{j}=f_{j}
for all
j=1,\ldots,r-2
,

it follows that we can then yield network formulations of the Coxeter-Toda Hamiltonians
H_{k}^{c,c}
in the above cases where the Coxeter-Toda Hamiltonians
H_{k}^{c,c}
can be expressed as linear combinations of the Hamiltonians
f_{i}^{c,c}
. When
G=SO_{2r}(\mathbb{C})
or
G=SO_{2r+1}(\mathbb{C})
, we can employ similar ideas in
[
Yan12
]
to yield network formulations of the Coxeter-Toda Hamiltonians arising from spin representations, though we will consider these Coxeter-Toda Hamiltonians separately at the end of this section.

To begin, we observe that there is only one elementary chip of type
E_{i}(a_{i})
, and only one elementary chip of type
E_{-i}(b_{i})
in
\overline{N}_{c,c}(\mathbf{i})
for all
i\in[1,r]
. Moreover, the first
r
elementary chips of
\overline{N}_{c,c}(\mathbf{i})
are of type
E_{-i}(b_{i})
, and the last
r
elementary chips of
\overline{N}_{c,c}(\mathbf{i})
are of type
E_{i}(a_{i})
. This implies that a path
P
in
\overline{N}_{c,c}(\mathbf{i})
whose start point is a source of
\overline{N}_{c,c}(\mathbf{i})
and whose end point is a sink of
\overline{N}_{c,c}(\mathbf{i})
is uniquely determined by the level where the start point lies, the level where the end point lies, and its lowest level. Henceforth, given a triple
(m,n,p)
with
m,p\geq n
, we say that
(m,n,p)
is an admissible
(c,c)
-triple, if there exists a (unique) path in
\overline{N}_{c,c}(\mathbf{i})
, which we will denote by
\overline{P}_{c,c}(m,n,p)
, whose start point lies on level
m
, whose lowest level is equal to
n
, and whose end point lies on level
p
, and we denote the set of admissible
(c,c)
-triples by
\overline{\mathcal{P}}^{c,c}
. Next, for any
m\geq n
, we say that
(m,n)
is an admissible
(c,c)
-pair, if
(m,n,m)
is an admissible
(c,c)
-triple, in which case we will write
P_{c,c}(m,n)
in lieu of
\overline{P}_{c,c}(m,n,m)
, and we denote the set of admissible
(c,c)
-pairs by
\mathcal{P}^{c,c}
.

Next, we define the weight
\wt(e)
of an edge
e
of
\overline{N}_{c,c}(\mathbf{i})
to be equal to the label of the edge
e
if
e
is labeled, and is equal to
1
otherwise. Also, given a path
P
in
\overline{N}_{c,c}(\mathbf{i})
whose start point is a source vertex and whose end point is a sink vertex, we define the weight
\wt(P)
of
P
to be equal to the product of the weights of the edges that constitute the path
P
. Then it follows that the
(i,j)
-th entry of
\overline{g}
is equal to the sum of the weights of the paths in
\overline{N}_{c,c}(\mathbf{i})
with source vertex labeled
i
and sink vertex labeled
j
.

Example 5.1
.

Let
G=SO_{5}(\mathbb{C})
. From Figure
11
, we see that
(5,1,1)
is not an admissible
(c,c)
-triple, as there are no paths in
\overline{N}_{c,c}(\mathbf{i})
whose start point lies on level
5
, and whose valleys and end point lie on level
1
. However,
(3,2,4)
is an admissible
(c,c)
-triple, and the path
\overline{P}_{c,c}(3,2,4)
has weight
\wt(\overline{P}_{c,c}(3,2,4))=\sqrt{2}c_{2}^{2}t_{2}^{2}/t_{1}
. The pair
(5,4)
is an admissible
(c,c)
-pair, as
(5,4,5)
is an admissible
(c,c)
-triple, and the path
P_{c,c}(5,4)
has weight
\wt(P_{c,c}(5,4))=c_{1}t_{1}/t_{2}^{2}
.

Given a subset
\overline{\mathcal{P}}=\{(m_{1},n_{1},p_{1}),\ldots,(m_{j},n_{j},p_{j})\}
of admissible
(c,c)
-triples with
m_{i}\neq m_{\ell}
and

for all
i\neq\ell
, we let

,

, and

G=SO_{5}(\mathbb{C})
, and

, it follows from Example
5.1
that we have
\wt_{c,c}(\overline{\mathcal{P}})=\sqrt{2}c_{1}c_{2}^{2}
.

Likewise, given a subset
\mathcal{P}=\{(m_{1},n_{1}),\ldots,(m_{j},n_{j})\}
of admissible
(c,c)
-pairs with
m_{i}\neq m_{\ell}
and
n_{i}\neq n_{\ell}
for all
i\neq\ell
, we may also define
s(\mathcal{P})
,
v(\mathcal{P})
and
\wt_{c,c}(\mathcal{P})
analogously.

For any subset of
\overline{\mathcal{P}}=\{(m_{1},n_{1},p_{1}),\ldots,(m_{i},n_{i},p_{i})\}

(c,c)

\overline{\mathcal{P}}

\overline{P}_{c,c}(m_{j},n_{j},p_{j})
and
\overline{P}_{c,c}(m_{k},n_{k},p_{k})
do not share any common vertices in
\overline{N}_{c,c}(\mathbf{i})
for any distinct
j,k\in[1,i]
. Similarly, for any subset of
\mathcal{P}=\{(m_{1},n_{1}),\ldots,(m_{i},n_{i})\}
of admissible
(c,c)
-pairs, we say that
\mathcal{P}
is non-intersecting if the paths
P_{c,c}(m_{j},n_{j})
and
P_{c,c}(m_{k},n_{k})
are non-intersecting for any distinct
j,k\in[1,i]
.

With this definition, we will define the set
\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{\nonint}^{c,c}(I)
for all

with
|I|=j
and
j\in[1,r]
as follows:

\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{\nonint}^{c,c}(I)=\{\overline{\mathcal{P}}\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{P}}^{c,c}:\overline{\mathcal{P}}\text{ is non-intersecting and }s(\overline{\mathcal{P}})=I=d(\overline{\mathcal{P}})\}.

Likewise, for all
I\subseteq[1,\dim V(\omega_{1})]
with
|I|=j
and
j\in[1,r]
, we may define
\mathcal{P}_{\nonint}^{c,c}(I)
analogously.

Given any
\overline{\mathcal{P}}=\{(m_{1},n_{1},p_{1}),\ldots,(m_{j},n_{j},p_{j})\}\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{\nonint}^{c,c}(I)
, we say that
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
is unmixed, if
m_{i}=p_{i}
for all
i=1,\ldots,j
, partially mixed if there exist distinct indices
i,\ell\in[1,j]
, such that
m_{i}=p_{i}
and
m_{\ell}\neq p_{\ell}
, and fully mixed otherwise.

Example 5.4
.

We let
G=SO_{5}(\mathbb{C})
. Then from Example
5.3
, the non-intersecting set
\overline{\mathcal{P}}=\{(3,2,4),(4,3,3)\}
is fully mixed.

Next, for any
\overline{\mathcal{P}}\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{\nonint}^{c,c}(I)
, we define
\sgn(\overline{\mathcal{P}})
by

\sgn(\overline{\mathcal{P}})=\sgn(\sigma),

where
\sigma
is the unique permutation in
S_{|I|}
that satisfy
p_{\sigma(i)}=m_{i}
for all
i=1,\ldots,|I|
. It is clear that
\sgn(\overline{\mathcal{P}})
is well-defined, as
\sgn(\overline{\mathcal{P}})
is independent of the choice of the ordering of elements of
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
. Then it follows from the Lindström-Gessel-Viennot lemma that we have

(5.2)

f_{j}^{c,c}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subseteq[1,\dim V(\omega_{1})],\\
|I|=j\end{subarray}}\sum_{\overline{\mathcal{P}}\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{\nonint}^{c,c}(I)}\sgn(\overline{\mathcal{P}})\wt_{c,c}(\overline{\mathcal{P}}).

for all
j=1,\ldots,r
. In particular, when
j=1
, we have

(5.3)

H_{1}^{c,c}=f_{1}^{c,c}=\sum_{(m,n)\in\mathcal{P}^{c,c}}\wt(P_{c,c}(m,n)).

While (
5.2
) does give us a network formulation of the Coxeter-Toda Hamiltonian
f_{j}^{c,c}
, this formulation is still too coarse for us to deduce explicit combinatorial formulas for
f_{j}^{c,c}
. As such, our goal in subsequent Subsections
5.2
-
5.5
is to refine (
5.2
), by further analyzing the structure of non-intersecting subsets
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
of admissible
(c,c)
-triples. This refinement will lead to a generalization of (
5.3
) for the other Coxeter-Toda Hamiltonians
f_{j}^{c,c}
,
j>1
, in that the Coxeter-Toda Hamiltonians
f_{j}^{c,c}
,
j>1
can be expressed as sums of weights of non-intersecting paths, each of which start and end on the same level.

5.2.
The Coxeter-Toda Hamiltonians of type
A

In this subsection, we will derive combinatorial formulas for the Coxeter-Toda Hamiltonians
H_{j}^{c,c}=f_{j}^{c,c}
, in the case where
G=SL_{r+1}(\mathbb{C})
. While these formulas were previously discovered by Di Francesco and Kedem
[
DFK09b
]
, the techniques that we employ to refine (
5.2
) in this case will be instructive in refining (
5.2
) for the other classical finite Dynkin types.

To begin, we have the following characterization of the non-intersecting subsets
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
of admissible
(c,c)
-triples in type
A
:

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
\overline{\mathcal{P}}=\{(m_{1},n_{1},p_{1}),\ldots,(m_{j},n_{j},p_{j})\}
be non-intersecting. Then
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
is unmixed, that is, we have
m_{i}=p_{i}
for all
i\in[1,j]
. In particular,
(m_{i},n_{i})
is an admissible
(c,c)
-pair for all
i=1,\ldots,j
, and
\sgn(\overline{\mathcal{P}})=1
.

Proof.

Suppose on the contrary that
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
is not unmixed. By removing paths
\overline{P}_{c,c}(m_{i},n_{i},p_{i})
in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
that satisfy
m_{i}=p_{i}
, we may assume that
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
is a fully mixed non-intersecting set. Without loss of generality, let us assume that
m_{1}<m_{2}<\cdots<m_{j}
, so that we have
m_{1}<p_{1}
. As we have
s(\overline{\mathcal{P}})=d(\overline{\mathcal{P}})
by assumption, there must exist some
\ell>1
, such that
m_{\ell}>p_{\ell}=m_{1}
.

As the elementary chip corresponding to
E_{-(m-1)}
appears before the elementary chip corresponding to
E_{-m}
in
\overline{N}_{c,c}(\mathbf{i})
for any
m\in[1,r-1]
,
m_{i}\geq n_{i}
, and
n_{i}
is the level on which the valleys of the path
\overline{P}_{c,c}(m_{i},n_{i},p_{i})
lie for all
i\in[1,j]
, it follows that we have either
n_{i}=m_{i}
or
n_{i}=m_{i}-1
for all
i\in[1,j]
, as shown in Figure
14
. In particular, we have
n_{\ell}\geq m_{\ell}-1
, which implies that
p_{\ell}\geq n_{\ell}\geq m_{\ell}-1\geq p_{\ell}
. Thus, equality must hold throughout, and we deduce that
m_{1}=p_{\ell}=n_{\ell}=m_{\ell}-1
, and so we must have
\ell=2
. Now, as we have
p_{1}\geq m_{1}+1=m_{2}
, this necessarily implies that the paths
\overline{P}_{c,c}(m_{1},n_{1},p_{1})
and
\overline{P}_{c,c}(m_{2},n_{2},p_{2})
must intersect at level
m_{1}
, as shown in Figure
15
, which contradicts the fact that
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
is non-intersecting. So
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
is unmixed as desired.
∎

As a consequence of Lemma
5.5
, it follows that (
5.2
) reduces to:

(5.4)

H_{j}^{c,c}=f_{j}^{c,c}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subseteq[1,r+1],\\
|I|=j\end{subarray}}\sum_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\nonint}^{c,c}(I)}\wt_{c,c}(\mathcal{P}).

involving levels

,

and

respectively.

Thus, our next step is to characterize the admissible
(c,c)
-pairs
(m,n)
, as well as when two paths
P_{c,c}(m,n)
and
P_{c,c}(m^{\prime},n^{\prime})
corresponding to two different admissible
(c,c)
-pairs
(m,n)
and
(m^{\prime},n^{\prime})
intersect.

Proposition 5.6
.

(1)

The admissible
(c,c)
-pairs are of the form
(i,i)
and
(j+1,j)
, where
i\in[1,r+1]
and
j\in[1,r]
.

(2)

For all
i\in[1,r+1]
and
j\in[1,r]
, the weights of the paths
P_{2i-1}:=P_{c,c}(i,i)
and
P_{2j}:=P_{c,c}(j+1,j)
are given by

(5.5)

\wt(P_{2i-1})=\frac{t_{i}}{t_{i-1}},\quad\wt(P_{2j})=\frac{c_{j}t_{j}}{t_{j-1}},

where
t_{0}=t_{r+1}=1
. In particular, these path weights
y_{i}:=\rho_{\tau}^{*}(\wt(P_{i}))
, written in terms of normalized
A_{r}^{(1)}

Q
-system variables using (
4.46
) and (
4.47
), are given by

(5.6)

y_{2i-1}=\frac{R_{i,1}R_{i-1,0}}{R_{i,0}R_{i-1,1}},\quad y_{2i}=\frac{R_{j-1,0}R_{j+1,1}}{R_{j,0}R_{j,1}},

where
R_{0,k}=R_{r+1,k}=1
for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(3)

The paths
P_{2i-1}=P_{c,c}(i,i)
and
P_{2i^{\prime}-1}=P_{c,c}(i^{\prime},i^{\prime})
do not intersect for any distinct
i,i^{\prime}\in[1,r+1]
, and for any
j\in[1,r]
and admissible
(c,c)
-pair
(m,n)\neq(j+1,j)
, the path
P_{2j}=P_{c,c}(j+1,j)
intersects
P_{k}=P_{c,c}(m,n)
if and only if
(m,n)=(j,j)
,
(j+1,j+1),(j,j-1)
or
(j+2,j+1)
, or equivalently,
k=2j\pm 1,2j\pm 2
.

Proof.

By the proof of Lemma
5.5
, we have that if
(m,n)
is an admissible
(c,c)
-pair, then either
m=n
or
m=n+1
, which proves statement (1). Statement (2) follows from the definition of path weights, and statement (3) follow from observation using Figure
16
.
