Remark
.

In particular, the formulas for the weights
y_{2i-1}
and
y_{2j}
given in (
5.6
) coincide with
[
DFK09b
, (3.9)]
.

The non-intersecting condition given in statement (3) of Proposition
5.6
can be described in terms of adjacency of vertices on a graph
\mathcal{G}_{A}
, where the graph
\mathcal{G}_{A}
is given as follows
[
DFK09b
, Section 3.3]
:
1
2
3
4
5
6
2r-2
2r-1
2r
2r+1

With the undirected graph
\mathcal{G}_{A}
given as above, it follows that for all distinct
j,k\in[1,2r+1]
, the paths
P_{j}
and
P_{k}
are non-intersecting if and only if vertices
j
and
k
are not adjacent to each other in
\mathcal{G}_{A}
.

Definition 5.7
.

A hard particle configuration
C
on
\mathcal{G}_{A}
is a subset of
[1,2r+1]
, such that if
j,k\in C
, then the vertices
j
and
k
are not adjacent to each other in
\mathcal{G}_{A}
. We denote the weight
\wt(C)
of the hard particle configuration
C
is defined by
\wt(C)=\prod_{i\in C}y_{i}
, and we denote the set of hard particle configurations on
\mathcal{G}_{A}
by
\HPC(\mathcal{G}_{A})
.

By (
5.4
), Lemma
5.5
, Proposition
5.6
and Definition
5.7
, we recover the following formula for the
j
-th conserved quantity
C_{j}=\rho_{\tau}^{*}(H_{j}^{c,c})
of the normalized
A_{r}^{(1)}

Q
-system, or equivalently, the
j
-th Coxeter-Toda Hamiltonian, written in terms of the normalized
A_{r}^{(1)}

Q
-system variables
[
DFK09b
, Theorem 3.10]
:

Theorem 5.8
.

Let
j\in[1,r]
. Then the
j
-th conserved quantity
C_{j}=\rho_{\tau}^{*}(H_{j}^{c,c})
of the normalized
A_{r}^{(1)}

Q
-system is given by

(5.7)

C_{j}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}C\in\HPC(\mathcal{G}_{A})\\
|C|=j\end{subarray}}\wt(C).

More precisely, we have

(5.8)

C_{j}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}<\cdots<i_{j}\leq 2r+1\\
i_{k}\leq i_{k+1}-2\text{ if }k\text{ is }odd,\\
i_{k}\leq i_{k+1}-3\text{ if }k\text{ is }even\end{subarray}}y_{i_{1}}\cdots y_{i_{j}}.

5.3.
The Coxeter-Toda Hamiltonians of type
C

In this subsection, we will derive combinatorial formulas for the Coxeter-Toda Hamiltonians
f_{j}^{c,c}
, in the case where
G=Sp_{2r}(\mathbb{C})
. Using the fact that
H_{2j}=\sum_{i=0}^{j}f_{2i}
and
H_{2\ell+1}=\sum_{i=0}^{\ell}f_{2i+1}
for all
j=1,\ldots,\left\lceil\frac{r-1}{2}\right\rceil
and
\ell=0,\ldots,\left\lfloor\frac{r-1}{2}\right\rfloor
, we will then have combinatorial formulas for the Coxeter-Toda Hamiltonians
H_{j}^{c,c}
in this case, which we will omit here.

To begin, we have the following characterization of the non-intersecting subsets
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
of admissible
(c,c)
-triples in type
C
:

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
\overline{\mathcal{P}}=\{(m_{1},n_{1},p_{1}),\ldots,(m_{j},n_{j},p_{j})\}
be non-intersecting. Then
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
is unmixed. In particular,
(m_{i},n_{i})
is an admissible
(c,c)
-pair for all
i=1,\ldots,j
, and
\sgn(\overline{\mathcal{P}})=1
.

Proof.

Suppose on the contrary that
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
is not unmixed. By removing paths
\overline{P}_{c,c}(m_{i},n_{i},p_{i})
in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
that satisfy
m_{i}=p_{i}
, we may assume that
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
is a fully mixed non-intersecting set. Without loss of generality, let us assume that
m_{1}<m_{2}<\cdots<m_{j}
, so that we have
m_{1}<p_{1}
. As we have
s(\overline{\mathcal{P}})=d(\overline{\mathcal{P}})
by assumption, there must exist some
\ell>1
, such that
m_{\ell}>p_{\ell}=m_{1}
. We shall arrive at a contradiction in steps.

Firstly, we assert that
s(\overline{\mathcal{P}})=d(\overline{\mathcal{P}})\subseteq[r+1,2r]
. Suppose on the contrary that this is not the case. Then by the minimality of
m_{1}
, we must have
n_{1}\leq m_{1}\leq r
. As the elementary chip corresponding to
E_{-(m-1)}
appears before the elementary chip corresponding to
E_{-m}
in
\overline{N}_{c,c}(\mathbf{i})
for any
m\in[1,r-1]
,
m_{i}\geq n_{i}
, and
n_{i}
is the level on which the valleys of the path
\overline{P}_{c,c}(m_{i},n_{i},p_{i})
lie for all
i\in[1,j]
, it follows from Figure
17
that if
m_{\ell}\geq r+1
, then we must have
n_{\ell}\geq r
, and if
m_{\ell}\leq r
, then we must have either
n_{\ell}=m_{\ell}
or
n_{\ell}=m_{\ell}-1
.

Now, if
m_{\ell}\geq r+1
and
n_{\ell}\neq r
, then this would necessarily imply that we have
p_{\ell}\geq n_{\ell}\geq r+1>r\geq m_{1}
, which contradicts the fact that
p_{\ell}=m_{1}
. Therefore, we must have
n_{\ell}=r
. As we have
p_{\ell}\geq n_{\ell}=r\geq m_{1}
, we must have equality to hold throughout, that is, we have
m_{1}=p_{\ell}=n_{\ell}=r
. As
m_{1}\leq r
, we must have either
n_{1}=m_{1}
or
n_{1}=m_{1}-1
, and by a similar argument as in the proof of Lemma
5.5
, it follows that the paths
\overline{P}_{c,c}(m_{1},n_{1},p_{1})
and
\overline{P}_{c,c}(m_{\ell},n_{\ell},p_{\ell})
must intersect at level
m_{1}=r
, which is a contradiction.

On the other hand, if
m_{\ell}\leq r
, then we must have
n_{\ell}\geq m_{\ell}-1
, which implies that
p_{\ell}\geq n_{\ell}\geq m_{\ell}-1\geq p_{\ell}
. Thus, equality must hold throughout, and we deduce that
m_{1}=p_{\ell}=n_{\ell}=m_{\ell}-1
, and so we must have
\ell=2
. Now, as we have
p_{1}\geq m_{1}+1=m_{2}
, it follows from a similar argument as in the proof of Lemma
5.5
that the paths
\overline{P}_{c,c}(m_{1},n_{1},p_{1})
and
\overline{P}_{c,c}(m_{2},n_{2},p_{2})
must intersect at level
m_{1}
, which is a contradiction. Therefore, we must have
s(\overline{\mathcal{P}})=d(\overline{\mathcal{P}})\subseteq[r+1,2r]
as claimed.

Next, we let
\sigma
be the unique permutation in
S_{j}
that satisfy
p_{\sigma(1)}<p_{\sigma(2)}<\cdots<p_{\sigma(j)}
, so that
p_{\sigma(1)}<m_{\sigma(1)}
, and we let
k>1
be the unique index satisfying
p_{\sigma(k)}>m_{\sigma(k)}=p_{\sigma(1)}
. As the elementary chip corresponding to
E_{m-1}(c_{m-1})
appears before the elementary chip corresponding to
E_{m}(c_{m})
in
\overline{N}_{c,c}(\mathbf{i})
for any
m\in[1,r-1]
, and we have
p_{i}\geq n_{i}
for all
i\in[1,j]
, it follows from Figure
18
that we must have either
n_{i}=p_{i}
or
n_{i}=p_{i}-1
for all
i\in[1,j]
. In particular, we have
n_{\sigma(k)}\geq p_{\sigma(k)}-1
, which implies that
m_{\sigma(k)}\geq n_{\sigma(k)}\geq p_{\sigma(k)}-1\geq m_{\sigma(k)}
. Thus, equality must hold throughout, and we deduce that
p_{\sigma(1)}=m_{\sigma(k)}=n_{\sigma(k)}=p_{\sigma(k)}-1
, and so this forces
k=2
.

, this necessarily implies that the paths

must intersect at level
p_{\sigma(2)}
, as shown in Figure

, which contradicts the fact that
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
is non-intersecting. So
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
is unmixed as desired.
∎

As a consequence of Lemma

, it follows that (
5.2
) reduces to:

(5.9)

f_{j}^{c,c}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subseteq[1,2r],\\
|I|=j\end{subarray}}\sum_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\nonint}^{c,c}(I)}\wt_{c,c}(\mathcal{P}).

Thus, our next step is to characterize the admissible
(c,c)
-pairs
(m,n)
, as well as when two paths
P_{c,c}(m,n)
and
P_{c,c}(m^{\prime},n^{\prime})
corresponding to two different admissible
(c,c)
-pairs
(m,n)
and
(m^{\prime},n^{\prime})
intersect.

Proposition 5.10
.

(1)

The admissible
(c,c)
-pairs are of the form
(i,i)
and
(j+1,j)
, where
i\in[1,2r]
and
j\in[1,2r-1]
.

(2)

For all
i\in[1,2r]
and
j\in[1,2r-1]
, the weights of the paths
P_{2i-1}:=P_{c,c}(i,i)
and
P_{2j}:=P_{c,c}(j+1,j)
are given by

(5.10)

\displaystyle\wt(P_{2k-1})

\displaystyle=\frac{t_{k}}{t_{k-1}},\quad\wt(P_{4r+1-2k})=\wt(P_{2k-1})^{-1}=\frac{t_{k-1}}{t_{k}},\quad k\in[1,r],

(5.11)

\displaystyle\wt(P_{2\ell})

\displaystyle=\frac{c_{\ell}t_{\ell}}{t_{\ell-1}},\quad\ell\in[1,r],

(5.12)

\displaystyle\wt(P_{4r-2\ell})

\displaystyle=\frac{c_{\ell}t_{\ell}}{t_{\ell+1}},\quad\ell\in[1,r-1],

where
t_{0}=1
. In particular, the path weights
y_{i}=\rho_{\tau}^{*}(\wt(P_{i}))
, written in terms of normalized
A_{2r-1}^{(2)}

Q
-system variables using (
4.46
) and (
4.47
), are given by

(5.13)

\displaystyle y_{2k-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{R_{k,1}R_{k-1,0}}{R_{k,0}R_{k-1,1}},\quad y_{4r+1-2k}=\frac{R_{k,0}R_{k-1,1}}{R_{k,1}R_{k-1,0}},\quad k\in[1,r],

(5.14)

\displaystyle y_{2\ell}

\displaystyle=\frac{R_{\ell-1,0}R_{\ell+1,1}}{R_{\ell,0}R_{\ell,1}},\quad y_{4r-2\ell}=\frac{R_{\ell-1,1}R_{\ell+1,0}}{R_{\ell,0}R_{\ell,1}},\quad\ell\in[1,r-1],

(5.15)

\displaystyle y_{2r}

\displaystyle=\frac{R_{r-1,0}R_{r-1,1}}{R_{r,0}R_{r,1}},

where
R_{0,k}=1
for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(3)

The paths
P_{2i-1}=P_{c,c}(i,i),P_{2i^{\prime}-1}=P_{c,c}(i^{\prime},i^{\prime})
do not intersect for any distinct
i,i^{\prime}\in[1,2r]
, and for any
j\in[1,2r-1]
and admissible
(c,c)
-pair
(m,n)\neq(j+1,j)
, the path
P_{2j}=P_{c,c}(j+1,j)
intersects
P_{k}=P_{c,c}(m,n)
if and only if
(m,n)=(j,j)
,
(j+1,j+1),(j,j-1)
or
(j+2,j+1)
, or equivalently,
k=2j\pm 1,2j\pm 2
.

Proof.

By the proof of Lemma
5.9
, we have that if
(m,n)
is an admissible
(c,c)
-pair, then either
m=n
or
m=n+1
, which proves statement (1). Statement (2) follows from the definition of path weights, and statement (3) follow from observation using Figures

,

and

.

i_{k}\leq i_{k+1}-3\text{ if }k\text{ is }even\end{subarray}}y_{i_{1}}\cdots y_{i_{j}}.

5.4.
The Coxeter-Toda Hamiltonians of type
B
arising from exterior powers of the defining representation

In this subsection, we will derive combinatorial formulas for the Coxeter-Toda Hamiltonians
H_{j}^{c,c}=f_{j}^{c,c}
,
j\in[1,r-1]
, in the case where
G=SO_{2r+1}(\mathbb{C})
. Unlike in types

and

, there exist fully mixed, non-intersecting subsets
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
of
\overline{\mathcal{P}}^{c,c}
, as Example
5.3
shows. Thus, our first goal in this subsection is to characterize the fully mixed non-intersecting subsets of
\overline{\mathcal{P}}^{c,c}
. To do so, we will proceed in several steps.

Lemma 5.12
.

Let
\overline{\mathcal{P}}=\{(m_{1},n_{1},p_{1}),\ldots,(m_{j},n_{j},p_{j})\}
be a fully mixed non-intersecting set, and

. Then
I\subseteq[r+1,2r+1]
, and
I\not\subseteq[r+3,2r+1]
.

Proof.

Firstly, we may argue in a similar way as in the first part of the proof of Lemma
5.9
, using Figure
22
where necessary, to deduce that
I\subseteq[r+1,2r+1]
. Subsequently, we may argue in a similar way as in the second part of the proof of Lemma
5.9
, using Figure
22
where necessary again, to deduce that
I\not\subseteq[r+3,2r+1]
. We will omit the details here.

Lemma 5.13
.

Let
\overline{\mathcal{P}}=\{(m_{1},n_{1},p_{1}),\ldots,(m_{j},n_{j},p_{j})\}
be a fully mixed non-intersecting set, and
I=\{m_{1},\ldots,m_{j}\}=\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{j}\}
. Then
r+1\in I
.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.12
, we must have either
r+1\in I
, or
r+2\in I
. Suppose on the contrary that we have
r+1\notin I
. As before, we may assume without loss of generality that we have
r+2=m_{1}<m_{2}<\cdots<m_{j}
. Then there exists some
\ell\in[2,j]
, such that
m_{\ell}>p_{\ell}=m_{1}=r+2
. As the elementary chip corresponding to
E_{r-2}(c_{r-2})
appears before the elementary chip corresponding to
E_{r-1}(c_{r-1})
in
\overline{N}_{c,c}(\mathbf{i})
, the elementary chip corresponding to
E_{r-1}(c_{r-1})
appears before the elementary chip corresponding to
E_{r}(c_{r})
in
\overline{N}_{c,c}(\mathbf{i})
, and we have
m_{1},p_{1}\geq n_{1}
, we must have
n_{1}=m_{1}=r+2
, and
p_{1}=m_{1}+1=r+3
, as shown in Figure
23
. As
m_{\ell}\geq r+3=p_{1}=m_{1}+1
, we must have
\ell=2
. Now, it is easy to observe using Figure
23
that the paths
\overline{P}_{c,c}(m_{1},n_{1},p_{1})=\overline{P}_{c,c}(r+2,r+2,r+3)
and
\overline{P}_{c,c}(m_{2},n_{2},p_{2})=\overline{P}_{c,c}(r+3,n_{2},r+2)
must intersect at level
r+2
during their descents, which contradicts the fact that
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
is non-intersecting. Therefore, we must have
r+1\in I
.

Corollary 5.14
.

Let
\overline{\mathcal{P}}=\{(m_{1},n_{1},p_{1}),\ldots,(m_{j},n_{j},p_{j})\}
be a fully mixed non-intersecting set. Then we have either
\overline{\mathcal{P}}=\{(r+1,r,r+2),(r+2,r+1,r+1)\}
or
\overline{\mathcal{P}}=\{(r+1,r+1,r+2),(r+2,r,r+1)\}
. In particular, we have
\sgn(\overline{\mathcal{P}})=-1
.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.13
, we may assume without loss of generality that
r+1=m_{1}<m_{2}<\cdots<m_{j}
. As the elementary chip corresponding to
E_{-(r-1)}
appears before the elementary chip corresponding to
E_{-r}
in
\overline{N}_{c,c}(\mathbf{i})
, it follows that we must have either
n_{1}=r+1
or
n_{1}=r
. Next, as the elementary chip corresponding to
E_{r-1}(c_{r-1})
appears before the elementary chip corresponding to
E_{r}(c_{r})
in
\overline{N}_{c,c}(\mathbf{i})
, it follows that in both cases, we must have
p_{1}=r+2
, which forces
m_{2}=r+2
.

Next, we will show that
p_{2}=r+1
. Suppose on the contrary that
p_{2}\neq r+1
. Then we must have
j>2
, so there must exist some
\ell\in[3,j]
, such that
p_{\ell}=r+1
. As the elementary chip corresponding to
E_{r-1}(c_{r-1})
appears before the elementary chips corresponding to
E_{r}(c_{r})
in
\overline{N}_{c,c}(\mathbf{i})
, we necessarily have
n_{2}=m_{2}=r+2
, but this would then imply that the paths
\overline{P}_{c,c}(m_{2},n_{2},p_{2})
and
\overline{P}_{c,c}(m_{\ell},n_{\ell},p_{\ell})
intersect at level
r+2
during their descents, as shown in Figure
24
, which contradicts the fact that
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
is non-intersecting. So
p_{2}=r+1
. Moreover, it follows that if
n_{1}=r+1
, then
n_{2}=r
, and if
n_{1}=r
, then
n_{2}=r+1
, as shown in Figure
25
.

Finally, suppose on the contrary that we have
j>2
. Then
\{(m_{3},n_{3},p_{3}),\ldots,(m_{j},n_{j},p_{j})\}
is also a fully mixed non-intersecting set, but
\{m_{3},\ldots,m_{j}\}\subseteq[r+3,2r+1]
, which contradicts the second part of Lemma
5.12
. Therefore, we have
\overline{\mathcal{P}}=\{(r+1,r,r+2),(r+2,r+1,r+1)\}
or
\overline{\mathcal{P}}=\{(r+1,r+1,r+2),(r+2,r,r+1)\}
as desired.
∎

.

(1)

(2)

Let

(5.18)

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{1}

\displaystyle=\{(r+1,r,r+2),(r+2,r+1,r+1)\},

(5.19)

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{2}

\wt_{c,c}(\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{1})=2c_{r}^{2}t_{r}^{2}/t_{r-1},\quad\wt_{c,c}(\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{2})=2c_{r}^{2}t_{r}^{2}/t_{r-1},\quad\wt_{c,c}(\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{3})=4c_{r}^{2}t_{r}^{2}/t_{r-1}

In particular, we have

(5.22)

\sum_{i=1}^{3}\sgn(\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{i})\wt_{c,c}(\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{i})=0.

(3)

For any admissible
(c,c)
-triple
(m,n,p)\notin\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{1}\cup\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{2}\cup\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{3}
, the following statements are equivalent:

(a)

\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{1}\cup\{(m,n,p)\}
is non-intersecting.

(b)

\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{2}\cup\{(m,n,p)\}
is non-intersecting.

(c)

\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{3}\cup\{(m,n,p)\}
is non-intersecting.

The proof of Lemma
5.15
follows from observing Figures
25
and
26
, and the proof shall be omitted.

As a consequence of Corollary
5.14
and Lemma
5.15
, it follows that (
5.2
) reduces to:

(5.23)

H_{j}^{c,c}=f_{j}^{c,c}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subseteq[1,2r+1],\\
|I|=j\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\nonint}^{c,c}(I)\\
\{(r+1,r),(r+2,r+1)\}\not\subseteq\mathcal{P}\end{subarray}}\wt_{c,c}(\mathcal{P}).

In view of Corollary
5.14
and Lemma
5.15
, our next step is to characterize the admissible
(c,c)
-pairs
(m,n)
, as well as when two paths
P_{c,c}(m,n)
and
P_{c,c}(m^{\prime},n^{\prime})
corresponding to two different admissible
(c,c)
-pairs
(m,n)
and
(m^{\prime},n^{\prime})
intersect.

Proposition 5.16
.

(1)

The admissible
(c,c)
-pairs
(m,n)
are of the form
(i,i)
,
(j+1,j)
and
(r+2,r)
, where
i\in[1,2r+1]
and
j\in[1,2r]
.

(2)

For all
i\in[1,r]\cup[r+2,2r+1]
and
j\in[1,2r]
, the weights of the paths
P_{2i-1}:=P_{c,c}(i,i)
,
P_{2j}:=P_{c,c}(j+1,j)
,
P_{(2r+1,1)}:=P_{c,c}(r+1,r+1)
and
P_{(2r+1,2)}:=P_{c,c}(r+2,r)
are given by

(5.24)

\displaystyle\wt(P_{2k-1})

\displaystyle=\frac{t_{k}}{t_{k-1}},\quad\wt(P_{4r+3-2k})=\wt(P_{2k-1})^{-1}=\frac{t_{k-1}}{t_{k}},\quad k\in[1,r-1],

(5.25)

\displaystyle\wt(P_{2r-1})

\displaystyle=\frac{t_{r}^{2}}{t_{r-1}},\quad\wt(P_{2r+3})=\wt(P_{2k-1})^{-1}=\frac{t_{r-1}}{t_{r}^{2}},

(5.26)

\displaystyle\wt(P_{2\ell})

\displaystyle=\frac{c_{\ell}t_{\ell}}{t_{\ell-1}},\quad\ell\in[1,r-1],

(5.27)

\displaystyle\wt(P_{4r+2-2\ell})

\displaystyle=\frac{c_{\ell}t_{\ell}}{t_{\ell+1}},\quad\ell\in[1,r-2],

(5.28)

\displaystyle\wt(P_{2r})

\displaystyle=\frac{2c_{r}t_{r}^{2}}{t_{r-1}},\quad\wt(P_{2r+2})=2c_{r},\quad\wt(P_{2r+4})=\frac{c_{r-1}t_{r-1}}{t_{r}^{2}},

(5.29)

\displaystyle\wt(P_{(2r+1,1)})

\displaystyle=1,\quad\wt(P_{(2r+1,2)})=\frac{c_{r}^{2}t_{r}^{2}}{t_{r-1}},

where
t_{0}=1
. In particular, the weights
y_{i}:=\rho_{\tau}^{*}(\wt(P_{i}))
,
y_{(2r+1,j)}:=\rho_{\tau}^{*}(\wt(P_{(2r+1,j)}))
, written in terms of normalized
D_{r+1}^{(2)}

Q
-system variables using (
4.46
) and (
4.47
), are given by

(5.30)

\displaystyle y_{2k-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{R_{k,1}R_{k-1,0}}{R_{k,0}R_{k-1,1}},\quad y_{4r+3-2k}=\frac{R_{k,0}R_{k-1,1}}{R_{k,1}R_{k-1,0}},\quad k\in[1,r-1],

(5.31)

\displaystyle y_{2r-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{R_{r,1}^{2}R_{r-1,0}}{R_{r,0}^{2}R_{r-1,1}},\quad y_{2r+3}=\frac{R_{r,0}^{2}R_{r-1,1}}{R_{r,1}^{2}R_{r-1,0}},

(5.32)

\displaystyle y_{2\ell}

\displaystyle=\frac{R_{\ell-1,0}R_{\ell+1,1}}{R_{\ell,0}R_{\ell,1}},\quad y_{4r+2-2\ell}=\frac{R_{\ell-1,1}R_{\ell+1,0}}{R_{\ell,0}R_{\ell,1}},\quad\ell\in[1,r-2],

(5.33)

\displaystyle y_{2r-2}

\displaystyle=\frac{R_{r-2,0}R_{r,1}^{2}}{R_{r-1,0}R_{r-1,1}},\quad y_{2r}=\frac{2R_{r-1,0}}{R_{r,0}^{2}},\quad y_{2r+2}=\frac{2R_{r-1,1}}{R_{r,1}^{2}},\quad y_{2r+4}=\frac{R_{r-2,1}R_{r,0}^{2}}{R_{r-1,0}R_{r-1,1}},

(5.34)

\displaystyle y_{(2r+1,1)}

\displaystyle=1,\quad y_{(2r+1,2)}=\frac{R_{r-1,0}R_{r-1,1}}{R_{r,0}^{2}R_{r,1}^{2}},

where
R_{0,k}=1
for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(3)

The paths
P_{2i-1}
and
P_{2i^{\prime}-1}
do not intersect for any distinct
i,i^{\prime}\in[1,r]\cup[r+2,2r+1]
, and the paths

and

do not intersect for any

.

(4)

, the path

if and only if

, and the path

.

(5)

.

.

.

(8)

.

intersects

if and only if

.

Proof.

By observing Figure
22
, we have that if
(m,n)
is an admissible
(c,c)
-pair, and either
m\leq r
or
m\geq r+3
, then we have either
m=n
or
m=n+1
. Thus, it remains to consider the case where either
m=r+1
or
m=r+2
. If
m=r+1
, then since the elementary chip corresponding to
E_{-(r-1)}
appears before the elementary chip corresponding to
E_{-r}
in
\overline{N}_{c,c}(\mathbf{i})
, it follows that we must have either
n=r+1
or
n=r
. By a similar reasoning, it follows that if
m=r+2
, then we must have either
n=r+2
,
n=r+1
or
n=r
, and this proves statement (1). Statement (2) follows from the definition of path weights, while statement (3) follows from the fact that the paths involved do not involve any ascents and descents. Statement (4) follow from observing Figures
16
and
21
, while modifying the labels that appear in Figure
16
to adapt to type
B
where necessary. Statements (5)–(9) follow from observing Figure
27
. We will leave the details to the reader.

∎

Motivated by (

) and statements (3)–(9) of Proposition

, let us define a graph
\mathcal{G}_{B}
, where the graph
\mathcal{G}_{B}
is given as follows:

that if vertices

. However, in light of Lemma

Similarly to types

and

, we may define a hard particle configuration on

, as well as the weight

of a hard particle configuration

By (

), Definition

, and Proposition

, we recover the following formula for the conserved quantity
C_{j}=\rho_{\tau}^{*}(H_{j}^{c,c})
of the normalized
D_{r+1}^{(2)}

-system, or equivalently, the Coxeter-Toda Hamiltonian
H_{j}^{c,c}
, written in terms of the normalized

Theorem 5.17
.

Let
j\in[1,r-1]
. Then the
j
-th conserved quantity

of the normalized

-system is given by

C_{j}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}C\in\HPC(\mathcal{G}_{B})\\

5.5.
The Coxeter-Toda Hamiltonians of type
D
arising from exterior powers of the defining representation

In this subsection, we will derive combinatorial formulas for the Hamiltonians
H_{j}^{c,c}=f_{j}^{c,c}
,
j\in[1,r-2]
, in the case where
G=SO_{2r}(\mathbb{C})
. Similar to type
B
, there exist fully mixed, non-intersecting subsets
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
of
\overline{\mathcal{P}}^{c,c}
. Thus, our first goal in this subsection is to characterize the fully mixed non-intersecting subsets of
\overline{\mathcal{P}}^{c,c}
. To do so, we will proceed in several steps.

Lemma 5.18
.

Let
\overline{\mathcal{P}}=\{(m_{1},n_{1},p_{1}),\ldots,(m_{j},n_{j},p_{j})\}
be a fully mixed non-intersecting set, and
I=\{m_{1},\ldots,m_{j}\}=\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{j}\}
. Then
I\subseteq[r,2r]
, and
I\not\subseteq[r+3,2r]
.

Proof.

Firstly, we may argue in a similar way as in the first part of the proof of Lemma
5.9
to deduce that
I\subseteq[r,2r]
. Subsequently, we may argue in a similar way as in the second part of the proof of Lemma
5.9
to deduce that
I\not\subseteq[r+3,2r]
. We will omit the details here.
∎

Lemma 5.19
.

Let
\overline{\mathcal{P}}=\{(m_{1},n_{1},p_{1}),\ldots,(m_{j},n_{j},p_{j})\}
be a fully mixed non-intersecting set, and
I=\{m_{1},\ldots,m_{j}\}=\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{j}\}
. Then
r\in I
.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.18
, we must have either
r\in I
,
r+1\in I
, or
r+2\in I
. Let us assume without loss of generality that we have
m_{1}<m_{2}<\cdots<m_{j}
. We let
k\in[2,j]
be the unique index satisfying
p_{k}=m_{1}
. Suppose on the contrary that we have
r\notin I
. Then we have either
r+1\in I
or
r+2\in I
. Let us first consider the case where
r+1\in I
, so that we have
p_{1}>m_{1}=r+1
. As the elementary chip corresponding to
E_{-(r-2)}
appears before the elementary chip corresponding to
E_{-r}
in
\overline{N}_{c,c}(\mathbf{i})
, and we have
p_{1}>m_{1}\geq n_{1}
, we must have
(m_{1},n_{1},p_{1})=(r+1,r-1,r+2)
or
(r+1,r+1,r+2)
, as shown in Figure
28
. In both cases, the paths
\overline{P}_{c,c}(m_{1},n_{1},p_{1})
and
\overline{P}_{c,c}(m_{k},n_{k},p_{k})=\overline{P}_{c,c}(m_{k},n_{k},r+1)
must intersect at level
m_{1}=r+1
during their descents, which contradicts the fact that
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
is non-intersecting. Therefore, we must have
r+1\notin I
, and hence we must have
r+2\in I
, so that we have
m_{1}=r+2
.

Corollary 5.20
.

Let
\overline{\mathcal{P}}=\{(m_{1},n_{1},p_{1}),\ldots,(m_{j},n_{j},p_{j})\}
be a fully mixed non-intersecting set. Then we have
\overline{\mathcal{P}}=\{(r,r-1,r+1),(r+1,r+1,r+2),(r+2,r,r)\}
. In particular, we have
\sgn(\overline{\mathcal{P}})=1
.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.19
, we may assume without loss of generality that
r=m_{1}<m_{2}<\cdots<m_{j}
. We let
k\in[2,j]
be the unique index satisfying
p_{k}=m_{1}=r
. Let us first show that
r+1\in I
. Suppose on the contrary that
r+1\notin I
. As the elementary chip corresponding to
E_{-(r-2)}
appears before the elementary chip corresponding to
E_{-r}
in
\overline{N}_{c,c}(\mathbf{i})
, and we have
p_{1}>m_{1}\geq n_{1}
, we must have
(m_{1},n_{1},p_{1})=(r,r-1,r+2)
or
(r,r,r+2)
, as shown in Figure
30
. In both cases, the paths
\overline{P}_{c,c}(m_{1},n_{1},p_{1})
and
\overline{P}_{c,c}(m_{k},n_{k},p_{k})=\overline{P}_{c,c}(m_{k},n_{k},r+1)
must intersect at level
m_{1}=r
during their ascents, which contradicts the fact that
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
is non-intersecting. Therefore, we must have
r+1\in I
, which forces
m_{2}=r+1
. In fact, our above argument would also show that
(m_{1},n_{1},p_{1})=(r,r-1,r+1)
.

Now, as the paths
\overline{P}_{c,c}(m_{1},n_{1},p_{1})=\overline{P}_{c,c}(r,r-1,r+1)
and
\overline{P}_{c,c}(m_{2},n_{2},p_{2})=\overline{P}_{c,c}(r+1,n_{2},p_{2})
do not intersect, we must have
n_{2}=m_{2}=r+1
, and hence
p_{2}=r+2
, as shown in Figure
31
. In particular, we have
r+2\in I
, and this forces
m_{3}=r+2
.

Next, we will show that
p_{3}=r
. Suppose on the contrary that
p_{3}\neq r
. Then we must have
j>3
, so there must exist some
\ell\in[4,j]
, such that
p_{\ell}=r
. As the elementary chip corresponding to
E_{r-2}(c_{r-2})
appears before the elementary chips corresponding to
E_{r-1}(c_{r-1})
and
E_{r}(c_{r})
in
\overline{N}_{c,c}(\mathbf{i})
, we necessarily have
n_{3}=m_{3}=r+2
, but this would then imply that the paths
\overline{P}_{c,c}(m_{3},n_{3},p_{3})
and
\overline{P}_{c,c}(m_{\ell},n_{\ell},p_{\ell})
intersect at level
r+2
during their descents, as shown in Figure
32
, which contradicts the fact that
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
is non-intersecting. So
p_{3}=r
, and this forces
n_{3}=r
. Consequently, we have
\{(r,r-1,r+1),(r+1,r+1,r+2),(r+2,r,r)\}\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{P}}
.

. Then

is also a fully mixed non-intersecting set, but
\{m_{4},\ldots,m_{j}\}\subseteq[r+3,2r]
, which contradicts the second part of Lemma

. Therefore, we have
\overline{\mathcal{P}}=\{(r,r-1,r+1),(r+1,r+1,r+2),(r+2,r,r)\}
as desired.

Lemma 5.21
.

(1)

The triple
(r+2,r-1,r+2)
is an admissible
(c,c)
-triple, and the path

(2)

Let

. Then

(5.36)

\wt_{c,c}(\overline{\mathcal{P}})=\frac{c_{r-1}c_{r}t_{r-1}t_{r}}{t_{r-2}}=\wt(\overline{P}_{c,c}(r+2,r-1,r+2))

(3)

For any admissible
(c,c)
-triple

, we have
\overline{\mathcal{P}}\cup\{(m,n,p)\}
is non-intersecting if and only if
\{(m,n,p),(r+2,r-1,r+2)\}
is non-intersecting.

The proof of Lemma
5.21
follows from observing Figures
33
and
34
, and the proof shall be omitted.

As a consequence of Corollary
5.20
, it follows that if
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
is a non-intersecting set with
|\overline{\mathcal{P}}|=j\geq 3
, then it is either fully mixed, in which case each of the paths corresponding to the admissible
(c,c)
-triples in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
start and end on the same level, or it is not fully mixed, in which case we can write
\overline{\mathcal{P}}=\overline{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}\sqcup\{(r,r-1,r+1),(r+1,r+1,r+2),(r+2,r,r)\}
, where
\overline{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
is a fully mixed non-intersecting set. By Lemma
5.21
, it follows that
\overline{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}\sqcup\{(r+2,r-1,r+2)\}
is also a fully mixed non-intersecting set with
j-2
elements, and
\wt_{c,c}(\overline{\mathcal{P}})=\wt_{c,c}(\overline{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}\sqcup\{(r+2,r-1,r+2)\})
. Consequently, it follows that (
5.2
) reduces to:

(5.37)

H_{j}^{c,c}=f_{j}^{c,c}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subseteq[1,2r],\\
|I|=j\end{subarray}}\sum_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\nonint}^{c,c}(I)}\wt_{c,c}(\mathcal{P})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subseteq[1,2r],\\
|I|=j-2\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{Q}\in\mathcal{P}_{\nonint}^{c,c}(I)\\
(r+2,r-1,r+2)\in\mathcal{Q}\end{subarray}}\wt_{c,c}(\mathcal{Q}).

In view of Corollary
5.20
and Lemma

, our next step is to characterize the admissible
(c,c)
-pairs

, as well as when two paths

intersect.

Proposition 5.22
.

(1)

(c,c)

For all

and

(5.38)

\displaystyle\wt(P_{2k-1})

\displaystyle=\frac{t_{k}}{t_{k-1}},\quad\wt(P_{4r+1-2k})=\wt(P_{2k-1})^{-1}=\frac{t_{k-1}}{t_{k}},\quad k\in[1,r]\setminus\{r-1\},

(5.39)

\displaystyle\wt(P_{2r-3})

\displaystyle=\frac{t_{r-1}t_{r}}{t_{r-2}},\quad\wt(P_{2r+3})=\wt(P_{2r-3})^{-1}=\frac{t_{r-2}}{t_{r-1}t_{r}},

(5.40)

\displaystyle\wt(P_{2\ell})

\displaystyle=\frac{c_{\ell}t_{\ell}}{t_{\ell-1}},\quad\wt(P_{4r-2\ell})=\frac{c_{\ell}t_{\ell}}{t_{\ell+1}}\quad\ell\in[1,r-3],

(5.41)

where
t_{0}=1
. In particular, the weights
y_{i}:=\rho_{\tau}^{*}(\wt(P_{i}))
,

, written in terms of normalized

-system variables using (

\displaystyle=\frac{R_{k,1}R_{k-1,0}}{R_{k,0}R_{k-1,1}},\quad y_{4r+1-2k}=\frac{R_{k,0}R_{k-1,1}}{R_{k,1}R_{k-1,0}},\quad k\in[1,r]\setminus\{r-1\},

\displaystyle=\frac{R_{\ell-1,0}R_{\ell+1,1}}{R_{\ell,0}R_{\ell,1}},\quad y_{4r-2\ell}=\frac{R_{\ell-1,1}R_{\ell+1,0}}{R_{\ell,0}R_{\ell,1}}\quad\ell\in[1,r-3],

\displaystyle=\frac{R_{r-2,0}R_{r-1,1}}{R_{r-1,0}R_{r,0}R_{r,1}},\quad y_{(2r,3)}=\frac{R_{r-2,1}R_{r-1,0}}{R_{r-1,1}R_{r,0}R_{r,1}},

\displaystyle=\frac{R_{r-2,0}R_{r-2,1}}{R_{r-1,0}R_{r-1,1}R_{r,0}R_{r,1}},

where
R_{0,k}=1
for all

.

(3)

The paths

.

, and the path

.

.

intersects

if and only if
k=2r-4,2r-3,2r-2,2r-1,(2r,1),(2r,3)
,
2r+1,2r+2,2r+3,2r+4
.

Proof.

By observing Figure
34
, we have that if
(m,n)
is an admissible
(c,c)
-pair, and either
m\leq r-1
or
m\geq r+3
, then we have either
m=n
or
m=n+1
. Thus, it remains to consider the case where either
m=r
,
m=r+1
or
m=r+2
. If
m=r
, then since the elementary chip corresponding to
E_{-(r-2)}
appears before the elementary chip corresponding to
E_{-(r-1)}
in
\overline{N}_{c,c}(\mathbf{i})
, it follows that we must have either
n=r
or
n=r-1
. By a similar reasoning, it follows that if
m=r+1
, then we must have either
n=r+1
or
n=r-1
. Finally, if
m=r+2
, then by a similar argument, we have
n=r+1,r
or
r-1
, and this proves statement (1).

Statement (2) follows from the definition of path weights, while statement (3) follows from the fact that the paths involved do not involve any ascents and descents. Statement (4) follow from observing Figures
16
and
21
, while modifying the labels that appear in Figure
16
to adapt to type
D
where necessary. Statements (5)–(11) follow from observation. We will leave the details to the reader.
∎

Motivated by statements (3)–(11) of Proposition
5.22
, let us define a graph
\mathcal{G}_{D}
, where the graph
\mathcal{G}_{D}
is given as follows:

Here, there are two complete graphs
K_{4}
as subgraphs of
\mathcal{G}_{D}
in the middle. The label
(4)
on the two thick lines indicates that the vertex
(2r,2)
connects to all
4
vertices in both complete graphs
K_{4}
. With the undirected graph
\mathcal{G}_{D}
given as above, it follows that for all distinct
j,k\in[1,2r-1]\cup[2r+1,4r-1]\cup\{(2r,j)\}_{j=1}^{3}
, we have that the paths
P_{j}
and

are non-intersecting if and only if vertices

are not adjacent to each other in
\mathcal{G}_{D}
.

Similarly to types

–

, we may define a hard particle configuration on

, as well as the weight
\wt(C)
of a hard particle configuration

on

, and the set of hard particle configurations

on

analogously.

By (
5.37
), Definition
5.7
, and Proposition
5.22
, we recover the following formula for the conserved quantity
C_{j}=\rho_{\tau}^{*}(H_{j}^{c,c})
of the normalized
D_{r}^{(1)}

Q
-system, or equivalently, the Coxeter-Toda Hamiltonian
H_{j}^{c,c}
, written in terms of the normalized
D_{r}^{(1)}

Q
-system variables:

Theorem 5.23
.

Let
j\in[1,r-2]
. Then the
j
-th conserved quantity
C_{j}=\rho_{\tau}^{*}(H_{j}^{c,c})
of the normalized
D_{r}^{(1)}

Q
-system is given by

(5.52)

C_{j}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}C\in\HPC(\mathcal{G}_{D})\\
|C|=j\end{subarray}}\wt(C)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}C\in\HPC(\mathcal{G}_{D})\\
|C|=j-2,(2r,2)\in C\end{subarray}}\wt(C).

5.6.
The Coxeter-Toda Hamiltonians arising from spin representations of special orthogonal groups

In this subsection, our goal is to provide combinatorial formulas for, as well as network formulations of, the Coxeter-Toda Hamiltonians
H_{r}^{c,c}
in the case where
G=SO_{2r+1}(\mathbb{C})
, as well as
H_{r-1}^{c,c}
and
H_{r}^{c,c}
in the case where
G=SO_{2r}(\mathbb{C})
. Our network formulations of these Coxeter-Toda Hamiltonians
H_{r}^{c,c}
will be similar with the network formulations of the
F
-polynomials in cluster algebras of classical finite types arising from spin representations derived in
[
Yan12
]
, and as such, we will use the notations and definitions developed therein for our network formulations of these Coxeter-Toda Hamiltonians for convenience.

Type
B

Our first goal in this subsubsection is to give a combinatorial formula for
H_{r}^{c,c}
. To begin, let us first recall the spin representation
V(\omega_{r})
of
G=SO_{2r+1}(\mathbb{C})
. To this end, we let
\mathcal{C}
denote the set of
r
-element subsets
I
of
[1,r]\cup[r+2,2r+1]
that satisfy
|I\cap\{k,2r+2-k\}|=1
for all
k\in[1,r]
. The spin representation
V(\omega_{r})
has a basis
\{v_{I}:I\in\mathcal{C}\}
of
\mathfrak{so}_{2r+1}
-weight vectors, indexed by elements of
\mathcal{C}
, with
v_{[1,r]}
being the highest weight vector. For each
k\in[1,r-1]
, we define

(5.53)

\displaystyle I_{k}^{+}

\displaystyle=\{I\in\mathcal{C}:k,2r+1-k\in I\},\quad I_{k}^{-}=\{I\in\mathcal{C}:k+1,2r+2-k\in I\}

(5.54)

\displaystyle I_{r}^{+}

\displaystyle=\{I\in\mathcal{C}:r\in I\},\quad I_{r}^{-}=\{I\in\mathcal{C}:r+2\in I\}.

We define a bijection
\phi_{k}:I_{k}^{+}\to I_{k}^{-}
for all
k\in[1,r]
by

(5.55)

\phi_{k}(I)=\begin{cases}(I\setminus\{k,2r+1-k\})\cup\{k+1,2r+2-k\}&\text{if }k\neq r,\\
(I\setminus\{r\})\cup\{r+2\}&\text{if }k=r.\end{cases}

The
\mathfrak{so}_{2r+1}
-action on
V(\omega_{r})
is then given by

(5.56)

\displaystyle e_{k}\cdot v_{I}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}v_{\phi_{k}^{-1}(I)}&\text{if }I\in I_{k}^{-},\\
0&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

(5.57)

\displaystyle e_{-k}\cdot v_{I}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}v_{\phi_{k}(I)}&\text{if }I\in I_{k}^{+},\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

In addition, for any
I\in\mathcal{C}
, we let

(5.58)

R_{I}^{\pm}=\{k\in[1,r]:I\in I_{k}^{\pm}\}.

Then it follows that as elements of
SL(V(\omega_{r}))
, we have

(5.59)

\displaystyle E_{k}(a)

\displaystyle=I+a\sum_{J\in I_{k}^{+}}e_{J,\phi_{k}(J)},

(5.60)

\displaystyle E_{-k}(b)

\displaystyle=I+b\sum_{J\in I_{k}^{+}}e_{\phi_{k}(J),J},

(5.61)

\displaystyle D(t_{1},\ldots,t_{r})

\displaystyle=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{C}}t_{I},

where

(5.62)

t_{I}=\frac{\prod_{i\in R_{I}^{+}}t_{i}}{\prod_{i\in R_{I}^{-}}t_{i}}.

We are now ready to compute
H_{r}^{c,c}
. Firstly, we note that for a given sequence
\mathbf{J}=(J_{0},\ldots,J_{2r})
in
\mathcal{C}
, we have

g_{\mathbf{J}}:=(E_{-1})_{J_{0},J_{1}}\cdots(E_{-r})_{J_{r-1},J_{r}}D(t_{1},\ldots,t_{r})_{J_{r},J_{r}}(E_{1}(c_{1}))_{J_{r},J_{r+1}}\cdots(E_{r}(c_{r}))_{J_{2r-1},J_{2r}}\neq 0

if and only if
J_{k}=J_{k-1}
or
J_{k}=\phi_{k}^{-1}(J_{k-1})
, and
J_{r+k}=J_{r+k-1}
or
J_{r+k}=\phi_{k}(J_{r+k-1})
for all
k\in[1,r]
, and in this case, we have

(5.63)

g_{\mathbf{J}}=t_{J_{r}}\prod_{k\in[1,r]:J_{r+k}=\phi_{k}(J_{r+k-1})}c_{k}.

Henceforth, we let
\mathcal{S}
denote the set of sequences
\mathbf{J}=(J_{0},J_{1},\ldots,J_{2r})
in
\mathcal{C}
satisfying
J_{0}=J_{2r}
,
J_{k}=J_{k-1}
or
J_{k}=\phi_{k}^{-1}(J_{k-1})
, and
J_{r+k}=J_{r+k-1}
or
J_{r+k}=\phi_{k}(J_{r+k-1})
for all
k\in[1,r]
. Then it follows that we have

(5.64)

H_{r}^{c,c}=\sum_{\mathbf{J}\in\mathcal{S}}g_{\mathbf{J}}.

Next, we claim that for any
J\in\mathcal{C}
, there is a bijection between the set
\mathcal{S}_{J}
of sequences
\mathbf{J}=(J_{0},J_{1},\ldots,J_{2r})
in
\mathcal{S}
with
J_{r}=J
, and the subsets of
R_{J}^{+}
. To begin, we first see from the definition of
I_{k}^{\pm}
and
R_{J}^{\pm}
that at most one of
i,i+1
can appear in
R_{J}^{\pm}
for all
i\in[1,r-1]
. This implies that for all distinct
i,j\in R_{J}^{+}
, we have
\phi_{i}(J)\in I_{j}^{+}
,
\phi_{j}(J)\in I_{i}^{+}
, and
(\phi_{i}\circ\phi_{j})(J)=(\phi_{j}\circ\phi_{i})(J)
. Based on this observation, it follows that for all
\mathbf{J}=(J_{0},J_{1},\ldots,J_{2r})
in
\mathcal{S}
with
J_{r}=J
, we have that if
J_{r+k}\neq J_{r+k-1}
, then
k\in R_{J}^{+}
. Hence, given a sequence
\mathbf{J}=(J_{0},J_{1},\ldots,J_{2r})
in
\mathcal{S}
with
J_{r}=J
, we have a subset
S_{\mathbf{J}}
of
R_{J}^{+}
, given by
S_{\mathbf{J}}=\{k\in[1,r]:J_{r+k}\neq J_{r+k-1}\}
.

Conversely, given a subset
S
of
R_{J}^{+}
, we may define
\mathbf{J}_{S}=(J_{0},J_{1},\ldots,J_{2r})\in\mathcal{S}_{J}
as follows: we first set
J_{r}=J
, and for all
i\in[1,r]
, we define
J_{i-1}=J_{i}
if
i\notin S
, and
J_{i-1}=\phi_{i}(J_{i})
otherwise. Similarly, we define
J_{r+i}=J_{r+i-1}
if
i\notin S
, and
J_{r+i}=\phi_{i}(J_{r+i-1})
otherwise. Then
\mathbf{J}_{S}
is a well-defined sequence in
\mathcal{C}
with
J_{r}=J
and
J_{0}=J_{2r}
, so we have
\mathbf{J}_{S}\in\mathcal{S}_{J}
. Now, it is easy to see that
\mathbf{J}_{S_{\mathbf{J}}}=\mathbf{J}
and
S_{\mathbf{J}_{S}}=S
, so this establishes a bijection between
\mathcal{S}_{J}
, and the subsets of
R_{J}^{+}
.

Now, using this established bijection, along with (
5.63
), we have

(5.65)

\sum_{\mathbf{J}\in\mathcal{S}_{J}}g_{\mathbf{J}}=t_{J}\prod_{k\in R_{J}^{+}}(1+c_{k}).

Combining this with (
5.64
), we have

(5.66)

H_{r}^{c,c}=\sum_{\mathbf{J}\in\mathcal{S}}g_{\mathbf{J}}=\sum_{J\in\mathcal{C}}\sum_{\mathbf{J}\in\mathcal{S}_{J}}g_{\mathbf{J}}=\sum_{J\in\mathcal{C}}t_{J}\prod_{k\in R_{J}^{+}}(1+c_{k}).

From (
5.66
), we deduce that:

Theorem 5.24
.

The
r
-th conserved quantity
C_{r}=\rho_{\tau}^{*}(H_{r}^{c,c})
of the normalized
D_{r+1}^{(2)}

Q
-system is given by

(5.67)

C_{r}=\sum_{J\in\mathcal{C}}z_{J}\prod_{k\in R_{J}^{+}}(1+Y_{k,1}),

where

(5.68)

z_{J}=\prod_{i\in R_{J}^{+}}\frac{R_{i,1}}{R_{i,0}}\prod_{i\in R_{J}^{-}}\frac{R_{i,0}}{R_{i,1}},\quad Y_{k,1}=\prod_{j=1}^{r}R_{j,1}^{-C_{j,k}}.

Our next goal in this subsubsection is to give a network formulation of
H_{r}^{c,c}
, or equivalently,
C_{r}
. Following
[
Yan12
]
, we say that a non-intersecting set
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
of admissible
(c,c)
-triples with
|\overline{\mathcal{P}}|=r
and
s(\overline{\mathcal{P}})=d(\overline{\mathcal{P}})
is bundled if the following conditions are satisfied:

(1)

s(\overline{\mathcal{P}})\in\mathcal{C}
,

(2)

None of the paths in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
intersect level
r+1
, and

(3)

For each
i\in[1,r-1]
, within each elementary chip that correspond to
E_{-i}
, either both of the diagonal edges belong to
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
, or neither of the diagonal edges belong to
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
, and within each elementary chip that correspond to
E_{i}(c_{i})
, either both of the diagonal edges belong to
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
, or neither of the diagonal edges belong to
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
.

In particular, it follows from the first condition that within the elementary chip that correspond to
E_{-r}
, none of the short diagonal edges belong to
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
, and within the elementary chip that correspond to
E_{r}(c_{r})
, none of the short diagonal edges belong to
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
. By Corollary
5.14
, it follows that
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
is necessarily unmixed, and
(r+1,r+1,r+1),(r+1,r,r+1),(r+2,r+1,r+2)\notin\overline{\mathcal{P}}
. Moreover, it follows from the bundled conditions that we must have
v(\overline{\mathcal{P}})\in\mathcal{C}
. In light of these observations, we say that a non-intersecting set
\mathcal{P}
of admissible
(c,c)
-pairs with
|\mathcal{P}|=r
is bundled if
s(\mathcal{P}),v(\mathcal{P})\in\mathcal{C}
. With this definition, we define
\mathcal{P}_{\nonint,\bund}^{c,c}(J)
for all
J\in\mathcal{C}
as follows:

(5.69)

\mathcal{P}_{\nonint,\bund}^{c,c}(J)=\{\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathcal{P}^{c,c}:\mathcal{P}\text{ is non-intersecting, }s(\mathcal{P})\in\mathcal{C}\text{ and }v(\mathcal{P})=J\}.

Now, it is easy to see that for any
J\in\mathcal{C}
, there is a natural bijection
\phi_{J}
between
\mathcal{P}_{\nonint,\bund}^{c,c}(J)
and
\mathcal{S}_{J}
. Moreover, due to the bundled conditions, it follows that for any
\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\nonint,\bund}^{c,c}(J)
, we have
\wt_{c,c}(\mathcal{P})=g_{\phi_{J}(\mathcal{P})}^{2}
. Consequently, it follows from (
5.64
) that we have

(5.70)

H_{r}^{c,c}=\sum_{J\in\mathcal{C}}\sum_{\mathbf{J}\in\mathcal{S}_{J}}g_{\mathbf{J}}=\sum_{J\in\mathcal{C}}\sum_{\mathcal{P}_{\nonint,\bund}^{c,c}(J)}\sqrt{\wt_{c,c}(\mathcal{P})}.

Finally, we may also give a combinatorial formula for
C_{r}
in terms of certain hard particle configurations
\mathcal{G}_{B}
. To begin, we shall define the notion of a bundled hard particle configuration on
\mathcal{G}_{B}
:

Definition 5.25
.

We say that a hard particle configuration
C
on
\mathcal{G}_{B}
is bundled if the following conditions are satisfied:

(1)

2r,2r+2,(2r+1,1)\notin C
,

(2)

Either
2k,4r+2-2k\in C
, or
2k,4r+2-2k\notin C
for all
k\in[1,r-1]
,

(3)

|C\cap\{1,4r,4r+1\}|=1
,
|C\cap\{2k-2,2k-1,4r+2-2k,4r+3-2k\}|=1
for all
k\in[2,r-1]
,
|C\cap\{2r-2,2r-1,(2r+1,2),2r+3\}|=1
, and

(4)

|C\cap\{1,2,4r+1\}|=1
,
|C\cap\{2k-1,2k,4r+3-2k,4r+4-2k\}|=1
for all
k\in[2,r-1]
, and
|C\cap\{2r-1,(2r+1,2),2r+3,2r+4\}|=1
.

We denote the set of bundled hard particle configurations on
\mathcal{G}_{B}
by
\HPC_{\bund}(\mathcal{G}_{B})
.

We claim that there is a natural bijection between
\HPC_{\bund}(\mathcal{G}_{B})
and
\bigcup_{J\in\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{P}_{\nonint,\bund}^{c,c}(J)
. Indeed, the first condition corresponds to the fact that
(r+1,r+1),(r+1,r),(r+2,r+1)\notin\mathcal{P}
, while the second condition corresponds to the fact that for each
i\in[1,r-1]
, within each elementary chip that correspond to
E_{-i}
, either both of the diagonal edges belong to
\mathcal{P}
, or neither of the diagonal edges belong to
\mathcal{P}
, and within each elementary chip that correspond to
E_{i}(c_{i})
, either both of the diagonal edges belong to
\mathcal{P}
, or neither of the diagonal edges belong to
\mathcal{P}
. The third condition corresponds to the fact that since
s(\mathcal{P})\in\mathcal{C}
, we have
|s(\mathcal{P})\cap\{k,2r+2-k\}|=1
for all
k\in[1,r]
, while the last condition corresponds to the fact that since
v(\mathcal{P})\in\mathcal{C}
, we have
|v(\mathcal{P})\cap\{k,2r+2-k\}|=1
for all
k\in[1,r]
.

By (
5.70
) and Definition
5.25
, we recover another formula for the
r
-th conserved quantity
C_{r}=\rho_{\tau}^{*}(H_{r}^{c,c})
of the normalized
D_{r+1}^{(2)}

Q
-system, or equivalently, the Coxeter-Toda Hamiltonian
H_{r}^{c,c}
, written in terms of the normalized
D_{r+1}^{(2)}

Q
-system variables:

Theorem 5.26
.

The
r
-th conserved quantity
C_{r}=\rho_{\tau}^{*}(H_{r}^{c,c})
of the normalized
D_{r+1}^{(2)}

Q
-system is given by

(5.71)

C_{r}=\sum_{C\in\HPC_{\bund}(\mathcal{G}_{B})}\sqrt{\wt(C)}.

Type
D

Our first goal in this subsubsection is to give a combinatorial formula for
H_{r-1}^{c,c}
and
H_{r}^{c,c}
. To begin, let us first recall the spin representations
V(\omega_{r-1})
and
V(\omega_{r})
of
G=SO_{2r}(\mathbb{C})
. To this end, we let
\mathcal{C}
denote the set of
r
-element subsets
I
of
[1,2r]
that satisfy
|I\cap\{k,2r+1-k\}|=1
for all
k\in[1,r]
. Then we have

(5.72)

\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{\odd}\sqcup\mathcal{C}_{\even},

where

(5.73)

\mathcal{C}_{\odd}=\{I\in\mathcal{C}:|I\cap[r+1,2r]|\text{ is odd}\},\quad\mathcal{C}_{\even}=\{I\in\mathcal{C}:|I\cap[r+1,2r]|\text{ is even}\}

The spin representations
V(\omega_{r-1})
and
V(\omega_{r})
has bases
\{v_{I}:I\in\mathcal{C}_{\odd}\}
and
\{v_{I}:I\in\mathcal{C}_{\even}\}
of
\mathfrak{so}_{2r+1}
-weight vectors, indexed by elements of
\mathcal{C}_{\odd}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\odd}
respectively, with
v_{[1,r-1]\cup\{r+1\}}
and
v_{[1,r]}
being the respective highest weight vectors. For each
k\in[1,r-1]
, we define

(5.74)

\displaystyle I_{k}^{+}

\displaystyle=\{I\in\mathcal{C}:k,2r-k\in I\},\quad I_{k}^{-}=\{I\in\mathcal{C}:k+1,2r+1-k\in I\}

(5.75)

\displaystyle I_{r}^{+}

\displaystyle=\{I\in\mathcal{C}:r-1,r\in I\},\quad I_{r}^{-}=\{I\in\mathcal{C}:r+1,r+2\in I\}.

We define a bijection
\phi_{k}:I_{k}^{+}\to I_{k}^{-}
for all
k\in[1,r]
by

(5.76)

\phi_{k}(I)=\begin{cases}(I\setminus\{k,2r-k\})\cup\{k+1,2r+1-k\}&\text{if }k\neq r,\\
(I\setminus\{r-1,r\})\cup\{r+1,r+2\}&\text{if }k=r.\end{cases}

The
\mathfrak{so}_{2r}
-action on both
V(\omega_{r-1})
and
V(\omega_{r})
is then given by formulas analogous to (
5.56
) and (
5.57
). By defining
R_{I}^{\pm}
for all
I\in\mathcal{C}
by (
5.58
), we see that as elements of
SL(V(\omega_{r-1}))
, we have

(5.77)

\displaystyle E_{k}(a)

\displaystyle=I+a\sum_{J\in I_{k}^{+}\cap\mathcal{C}_{\odd}}e_{J,\phi_{k}(J)},

(5.78)

\displaystyle E_{-k}(b)

\displaystyle=I+b\sum_{J\in I_{k}^{+}\cap\mathcal{C}_{\odd}}e_{\phi_{k}(J),J},

(5.79)

\displaystyle D(t_{1},\ldots,t_{r})

\displaystyle=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{C}_{\odd}}t_{I},

and as elements of
SL(V(\omega_{r}))
, we have

(5.80)

\displaystyle E_{k}(a)

\displaystyle=I+a\sum_{J\in I_{k}^{+}\cap\mathcal{C}_{\even}}e_{J,\phi_{k}(J)},

(5.81)

\displaystyle E_{-k}(b)

\displaystyle=I+b\sum_{J\in I_{k}^{+}\cap\mathcal{C}_{\even}}e_{\phi_{k}(J),J},

(5.82)

\displaystyle D(t_{1},\ldots,t_{r})

\displaystyle=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{C}_{\even}}t_{I},

where
t_{I}
is defined by (
5.62
).

We are now ready to compute
H_{r-1}^{c,c}
and
H_{r}^{c,c}
. As in type
B
, we let
\mathcal{S}_{\odd}
(respectively
\mathcal{S}_{\even}
) denote the set of sequences
\mathbf{J}=(J_{0},J_{1},\ldots,J_{2r})
in
\mathcal{C}_{\odd}
(respectively
\mathcal{C}_{\even}
) satisfying
J_{0}=J_{2r}
,
J_{k}=J_{k-1}
or
J_{k}=\phi_{k}^{-1}(J_{k-1})
, and
J_{r+k}=J_{r+k-1}
or
J_{r+k}=\phi_{k}(J_{r+k-1})
for all
k\in[1,r]
. Then it follows that we have

(5.83)

H_{r-1}^{c,c}=\sum_{\mathbf{J}\in\mathcal{S}_{\odd}}g_{\mathbf{J}},\quad H_{r}^{c,c}=\sum_{\mathbf{J}\in\mathcal{S}_{\even}}g_{\mathbf{J}},

where
g_{\mathbf{J}}
is defined by (
5.63
). As in type
B
, we have that for any
J\in\mathcal{C}_{\odd}
(respectively
J\in\mathcal{C}_{\even}
), there is a bijection between the set
\mathcal{S}_{J}
of sequences
\mathbf{J}=(J_{0},J_{1},\ldots,J_{2r})
in
\mathcal{S}_{\odd}
(respectively
\mathcal{S}_{\even}
) with
J_{r}=J
, and the subsets of
R_{J}^{+}
. Thus, by a similar argument as in type
B
, it follows that we have

(5.84)

H_{r-1}^{c,c}=\sum_{J\in\mathcal{C}_{\odd}}t_{J}\prod_{k\in R_{J}^{+}}(1+c_{k}),\quad H_{r}^{c,c}=\sum_{J\in\mathcal{C}_{\even}}t_{J}\prod_{k\in R_{J}^{+}}(1+c_{k})

Thus, from (
5.84
), we deduce that:

Theorem 5.27
.

The
(r-1)
-th and
r
-th conserved quantities
C_{r-1}=\rho_{\tau}^{*}(H_{r-1}^{c,c})
and
C_{r}=\rho_{\tau}^{*}(H_{r}^{c,c})
of the normalized
D_{r}^{(1)}

Q
-system are given by

(5.85)

C_{r-1}=\sum_{J\in\mathcal{C}_{\odd}}z_{J}\prod_{k\in R_{J}^{+}}(1+Y_{k,1}),\quad C_{r}=\sum_{J\in\mathcal{C}_{\even}}z_{J}\prod_{k\in R_{J}^{+}}(1+Y_{k,1})

where
z_{J}
and
Y_{k,1}
are given by (
5.68
).

Our next goal in this subsubsection is to give network formulations of
H_{r-1}^{c,c}
and
H_{r}^{c,c}
, or equivalently,
C_{r-1}
and
C_{r}
. Following
[
Yan12
]
, we say that a non-intersecting set
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
of admissible
(c,c)
-triples with
|\overline{\mathcal{P}}|=r
and
s(\overline{\mathcal{P}})=d(\overline{\mathcal{P}})
is bundled if the following conditions are satisfied:

(1)

s(\overline{\mathcal{P}})\in\mathcal{C}
,

(2)

For each
i\in[1,r]
, within each elementary chip that correspond to
E_{-i}
, either both of the diagonal edges belong to
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
, or neither of the diagonal edges belong to
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
, and within each elementary chip that correspond to
E_{i}(c_{i})
, either both of the diagonal edges belong to
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
, or neither of the diagonal edges belong to
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
.

In particular, it follows from the bundled conditions, along with Corollary
5.20
that
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
is necessarily unmixed,
(r+2,r+1,r+2)\notin\overline{\mathcal{P}}
, and
v(\overline{\mathcal{P}})\in\mathcal{C}
. In light of these observations, we say that a non-intersecting set
\mathcal{P}
of admissible
(c,c)
-pairs with
|\mathcal{P}|=r
is bundled if
s(\mathcal{P}),v(\mathcal{P})\in\mathcal{C}
. With this definition, we define
\mathcal{P}_{\nonint,\bund}^{c,c}(J)
analogously by (
5.69
) for all
J\in\mathcal{C}
.

Now, it is easy to see that for any
J\in\mathcal{C}
, there is a natural bijection
\phi_{J}
between
\mathcal{P}_{\nonint,\bund}^{c,c}(J)
and
\mathcal{S}_{J}
. Moreover, due to the bundled conditions, it follows that for any
\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\nonint,\bund}^{c,c}(J)
, we have
\wt_{c,c}(\mathcal{P})=g_{\phi_{J}(\mathcal{P})}^{2}
. Consequently, it follows from (
5.84
) that we have

(5.86)

H_{r-1}^{c,c}=\sum_{J\in\mathcal{C}_{\odd}}\sum_{\mathcal{P}_{\nonint,\bund}^{c,c}(J)}\sqrt{\wt_{c,c}(\mathcal{P})},\quad H_{r}^{c,c}=\sum_{J\in\mathcal{C}_{\even}}\sum_{\mathcal{P}_{\nonint,\bund}^{c,c}(J)}\sqrt{\wt_{c,c}(\mathcal{P})}.

Finally, we may also give combinatorial formulas for
C_{r-1}
and
C_{r}
in terms of certain hard particle configurations
\mathcal{G}_{D}
. To begin, we shall define the notion of a bundled hard particle configuration on
\mathcal{G}_{D}
:

Definition 5.28
.

We say that a hard particle configuration
C
on
\mathcal{G}_{D}
is bundled if the following conditions are satisfied:

(1)

(2r,2)\notin C
,

(2)

Either
2k,4r-2k\in C
, or
2k,4r-2k\notin C
for all
k\in[1,r-1]
, and either
(2r,1),(2r,3)\in C
,
(2r,1),(2r,3)\notin C
,

(3)

|C\cap\{1,4r-2,4r-1\}|=1
,
|C\cap\{2k-2,2k-1,4r-2k,4r+1-2k\}|=1
for all
k\in[2,r-2]
,
|C\cap\{2r-4,2r-3,(2r,3),2r+2,2r+3\}|=1
,
|C\cap\{2r-2,2r-1,(2r,1),2r+1\}|=1
and

(4)

|C\cap\{1,2,4r-1\}|=1
,
|C\cap\{2k-1,2k,4r+1-2k,4r+2-2k\}|=1
for all
k\in[2,r-2]
,
|C\cap\{2r-3,2r-2,(2r,1),2r+3,2r+4\}|=1
and
|C\cap\{2r-1,(2r,3),2r+1,2r+2\}|=1
.

We denote the set of bundled hard particle configurations
C
on
\mathcal{G}_{B}
for which
|C\cap([2r+1,4r-1]\cup\{(2r,1),(2r,3)\})|
is odd by
\HPC_{\bund,\odd}(\mathcal{G}_{D})
, and we denote the set of bundled hard particle configurations
C
on
\mathcal{G}_{B}
for which
|C\cap([2r+1,4r-1]\cup\{(2r,1),(2r,3)\})|
is even by
\HPC_{\bund,\even}(\mathcal{G}_{D})
.

We claim that there is a natural bijection between
\HPC_{\bund,\odd}(\mathcal{G}_{B})
and
\bigcup_{J\in\mathcal{C}_{\odd}}\mathcal{P}_{\nonint,\bund}^{c,c}(J)
, as well as the sets
\HPC_{\bund,\even}(\mathcal{G}_{B})
and
\bigcup_{J\in\mathcal{C}_{\even}}\mathcal{P}_{\nonint,\bund}^{c,c}(J)
. Indeed, the first condition corresponds to the fact that
(r+2,r-1),\notin\mathcal{P}
, while the second condition corresponds to the fact that for each
i\in[1,r]
, within each elementary chip that correspond to
E_{-i}
, either both of the diagonal edges belong to
\mathcal{P}
, or neither of the diagonal edges belong to
\mathcal{P}
, and within each elementary chip that correspond to
E_{i}(c_{i})
, either both of the diagonal edges belong to
\mathcal{P}
, or neither of the diagonal edges belong to
\mathcal{P}
. The third condition corresponds to the fact that since
s(\mathcal{P})\in\mathcal{C}
, we have
|s(\mathcal{P})\cap\{k,2r+1-k\}|=1
for all
k\in[1,r]
, while the last condition corresponds to the fact that since
v(\mathcal{P})\in\mathcal{C}
, we have
|v(\mathcal{P})\cap\{k,2r+1-k\}|=1
for all
k\in[1,r]
.

By (
5.86
) and Definition
5.28
, we record another formula for the
(r-1)
-th and
r
-th conserved quantities
C_{r-1}=\rho_{\tau}^{*}(H_{r-1}^{c,c})
and
C_{r}=\rho_{\tau}^{*}(H_{r}^{c,c})
of the normalized
D_{r}^{(1)}

Q
-system, or equivalently, the Coxeter-Toda Hamiltonians
H_{r-1}^{c,c}
and
H_{r}^{c,c}
, written in terms of the normalized
D_{r}^{(1)}

Q
-system variables:

Theorem 5.29
.

The
(r-1)
-th and
r
-th conserved quantities
C_{r-1}=\rho_{\tau}^{*}(H_{r-1}^{c,c})
and
C_{r}=\rho_{\tau}^{*}(H_{r}^{c,c})
of the normalized
D_{r}^{(1)}

Q
-system are given by

(5.87)

C_{r-1}=\sum_{C\in\HPC_{\bund,\odd}(\mathcal{G}_{D})}\sqrt{\wt(C)},\quad C_{r}=\sum_{C\in\HPC_{\bund,\even}(\mathcal{G}_{D})}\sqrt{\wt(C)}.

6.
Conclusion

In this paper, we constructed generalized Bäcklund-Darboux transformations between two conjugation quotient Coxeter double Bruhat cells in terms of cluster mutations, and shown that these transformations preserve Coxeter-Toda Hamiltonians arising from trace functions of representations of a simple Lie group
4.4
, which implies that these generalized Bäcklund-Darboux transformations preserve Hamiltonian flows generated by the aforementioned Coxeter-Toda Hamiltonians
4.3
, thereby generalizing
[
GSV11
, Theorem 6.1]
to the other finite Lie types. In particular, this shows that the family of Coxeter-Toda systems on a simple Lie group forms a single cluster integrable system.

In addition, we have also developed network formulations of Coxeter-Toda Hamiltonians arising from trace functions of the fundamental representations or the exterior powers of the defining representation in the case where we have a simple classical Lie group, using the network formulations of elements that we have developed at the end of Section
2
. By further examining the possible collections of non-intersecting paths that can arise in each classical Dynkin type, and using the connection between the discrete dynamics of the factorization mapping defined in (
4.37
) and
Q
-system evolutions established in
[
GSV11
,
Wil15
]
, we provide combinatorial formulas for Coxeter-Toda Hamiltonians of types
A_{r},B_{r},C_{r}
and
D_{r}
in terms of partition functions of hard particles on weighted graphs
\mathcal{G}_{A},\mathcal{G}_{B},\mathcal{G}_{C},\mathcal{G}_{D}
respectively, or equivalently, conserved quantities of
Q
-systems of affine Dynkin types
A_{r}^{(1)},D_{r+1}^{(2)},A_{2r-1}^{(2)}
and
D_{r}^{(1)}
respectively, given in Theorems
5.8
,
5.11
,
5.17
,
5.23
,
5.24
and
5.27
.

Here, we would like to mention a few directions for future work. Firstly, owing to the fact that there exists a natural Poisson structure on a given cluster algebra that is compatible with the cluster structure
[
GSV03
]
, it follows that cluster algebras admit natural quantizations
[
BZ05
]
, whose classical limit coincides with the aforementioned Poisson structure. Thus, it is a natural question to ask if we can extend the network formulations of the Coxeter-Toda Hamiltonians for a simple classical Lie group to the quantum case as well, and if we can derive combinatorial formulas for the quantum Coxeter-Toda Hamiltonians for a simple classical Lie group. As a first step in this direction, Lunsford
[
Lun23
]
obtained network formulations of quantum Coxeter-Toda Hamiltonians of types
A
and
C
, and identified them with the corresponding quantum difference Toda Hamiltonians of types
A
and
C
respectively obtained by Gonin and Tsymbaliuk
[
GT21
]
using the Lax formalism.

Next, we would like to remark that cluster structures on double Bruhat cells of affine Kac-Moody groups were studied in
[
Wil13a
]
. In addition, a rigorous treatment of infinite-dimensional Poisson-Lie theory was developed in
[
Wil13b
]
, which allows one to define Poisson structures on affine Kac-Moody groups that is compatible with the underlying cluster structures, and subsequently construct affine Coxeter-Toda systems. Thus, it is natural to ask if there exist generalized Bäcklund-Darboux transformations between two conjugation quotient Coxeter double Bruhat cells of an affine Kac-Moody group that preserve flows generated by an affine Coxeter-Toda Hamiltonian, and if we can extend the network formulations of the Coxeter-Toda Hamiltonians for a simple classical Lie group to the affine case as well.

Finally, we would like to mention that while the
Q
-systems of types
B_{r}^{(1)}
and
C_{r}^{(1)}
admit cluster algebraic formulations as well
[
DFK09a
]
, we did not consider these
Q
-systems in this paper, as we were unable to make a direct connection between the dynamics of these
Q
-system evolutions and that of factorization mappings. It is a natural question to ask if the conserved quantities of these
Q
-systems admit network formulations as well, and if any such network formulation of the conserved quantities of the
B_{r}^{(1)}

Q
-system has any connection with the formulation of the conserved quantities of the
B_{r}^{(1)}

Q
-system given in terms of partition functions of weights of perfect matchings on a weighted torus graph given in
[
Vic18
]
.
